#3011. The race is on to build the first fusion power plant — and Europe is looking to Japan for help

The race is on to build the world's first commercial fusion power plant — and one of Europe's leading contenders in the field has just received a boost.

Munich-based Proxima Fusion signed memoranda of understanding with two Japanese industrial partners, Fujikura and Kyoto Fusioneering, in Tokyo on Oct. 28. Japanese Prime Minister Sanae Takaichi and German Chancellor Friedrich Merz — who was visiting in a bid to deepen trade ties — were both in attendance.

Ann Mettler, president of Catalyse Europe, a Brussels-based clean-energy think tank, and a former vice president of Breakthrough Energy, said developing the technology should be a strategic imperative for Europe.

"Europe is a resource-poor geography and we are very dependent on China. The world is in competition for technological supremacy — this is a zero-sum game," Mettler told CNBC, speaking in a personal capacity.

"Fusion is a silver bullet because the promise is abundant, clean, limitless energy."

Fusion energy seeks to replicate the way the sun produces power, using super-heated plasma to fuse two light atomic nuclei into one heavier one, releasing vast amounts of energy in the process.

There are two main approaches: one uses high-powered lasers, while the other uses powerful magnets to confine the plasma. Proxima is developing a type of magnetic device called a stellarator, which is designed to hold the plasma in place for long periods.

The agreement with Kyoto Fusioneering will expand existing work on next-generation gyrotrons — devices that generate high-power microwaves used to heat the plasma.

Proxima's agreement with 141-year-old Fujikura will explore plans for industrial-scale manufacturing in Germany of high-temperature superconducting, or HTS, tape, a specialized metallic tape that is superconducting at relatively high temperatures and can carry electrical current with virtually no resistive losses.

The tape is wound into coils to form powerful magnets that confine and control the heated plasma inside the stellarator.

Francesco Sciortino, CEO and co-founder of Proxima Fusion, told CNBC that Fujikura, which employs over 50,000 people worldwide, was a leader in manufacturing high-temperature superconductors, having built its reputation in optical fibers. "They think this tape has the potential to be the next optical fibre," he said.

He said Proxima, which is studying plans to build a manufacturing facility in Lower Saxony, hoped to make around 5,000 kilometers, or 3,100 miles, of tape annually by 2030.

The 140 million euro ($160 million) facility would be financed partly by the proceeds of a $468 million funding round in July, which valued Proxima, whose backers include Alphabet-owned Google, at $2.7 billion.

Proxima expects to need around 20,000 kilometers (12,400 miles) of the tape for its "Alpha" fusion demonstrator and a further 40,000 kilometers (24,900 miles) for its planned "Stellaris" fusion power station.

Sciortino said that establishing production of the tape in Germany was crucial as it would help Europe create the expertise and supply chains needed to turn its fusion research leadership into industrial capability.

This is where Europe has historically struggled, according to Catalyse Europe's Mettler.

"Every geography wants to turn leadership in science into industrial leadership and this is where Europe has traditionally been not so good — turning that scientific leadership into euros," she said.

While Europe had industrial giants to be proud of, disruptors tended to be more successful in frontier technologies, according to Mettler.

"If Europe is first to develop this technology, it will give us a huge advantage, but key to that will be building the supply chain," she said.

Sciortino said he remains confident Proxima will be the first company to turn on a pilot plant — but stressed the key to profitability would be making that plant as simple as possible to operate.

"You can have complexity in the design, manufacturing and operation of the plant," he said. "We can take care of the complexity in the design and manufacturing, but our view is that there needs to be simplicity in the operation. We are aiming for net energy gain during the early 2030s."

But Proxima has some well-heeled competitors. They include U.S.-based Commonwealth Fusion Systems, which is also backed by Google, and Helion Energy, as well as Canada's General Fusion. China has a number of state-backed players, most notably China Fusion Energy Co., while Tokyo-based Helical Fusion leads Japan's push.

"The supply chain in fusion energy needs to be built years before — people often think of frontier technologies as a switch that can be thrown," Mettler said. "That is not the case here."

#3007. Mississippi Senate: Longshot Ty Pinkins says he aimed to spoil Hyde-Smith, boost Colom vote

Longshot Mississippi Senate candidate Ty Pinkins said that at first wanted to win — but that he stayed in to spoil.

Pinkins told a local TV station that after an April poll showed he had no chance of winning he remained in the race in order to boost Democratic nominee Scott Colom's chances of beating Republican incumbent Sen. Cindy Hyde-Smith.

Pinkins' purported strategy might pay off.

Colom is narrowly leading Hyde-Smith in ongoing counting by just 1,936 votes, a margin of just 0.2 percentage points, with 96.2% of the vote counted, according to the Associated Press. That margin is about one-tenth the size of Pinkins' share of the vote, 2.1 percentage points.

Pinkins has won just 20,010 votes so far out of the nearly 952,000 votes already counted.

"The only thing I did was stay in the race and let people shoot arrows at me," Pinkins said in an interview with WTVA in Mississippi on Thursday. "That is it. And that's a ... low price to pay to get someone in this race that I think can help people in the Delta and all across this state."

"The portion of the vote that I drew — and people were concerned about splitting the Black vote — what I actually did was split the Republican vote," he said.

Pinkins told the station in Tupelo that he intentionally avoided campaigning in areas with large Black populations to avoid cutting into the vote share of Colom, who, like Pinkins, is Black.

That tactic worked, he said, as the election results show he got most of his support from areas with relatively low Black populations.

"I'll be the boogeyman," he said. "But I'll tell you this: The independent in this race pulled most of his votes from a Republican, and for the first time since Reconstruction, a Democrat who is a Black man is leading."

Pinkins, a lawyer who previously ran for Mississippi secretary of state, was the Democratic nominee for Senate in 2024. He was crushed by Republican Sen. Roger Wicker, who won nearly 63% of the vote, compared with about 37% for Pinkins.

Despite that showing, the U.S. Army veteran decided to take another crack at the Senate in 2026.

But in the spring, he saw the writing on the wall for his candidacy.

"I got in this race to win," Pinkins told WTVA. "In April, I knew I was going to lose, but I also knew that I was pulling 70% of the Republican vote based on the [SPLC Action Fund and Impact Research] poll."

SPLC Action Fund, when it released the poll, said, "Sagging favorability numbers for Sen. Cindy Hyde-Smith could open up a path to victory for Democrats."

"Independent Ty Pinkins has the potential to play spoiler for either candidate," SPLC Action Fund said in April.

In the WTVA interview, Pinkins said, "I probably attended maybe, maybe six events over the summer" as part of his campaign.

"Six events. I don't have to," Pinkins said. "Why? I'm not going to win. So why go to these Black communities that he's going to?"

Pinkins said all he had to do was visit counties that have relatively low Black populations and "talk to some Republicans that said they're going to vote for me."

"They were literally calling me and sending me emails: 'We're going to vote for you because we don't want to waste our vote on Cindy Hyde-Smith, we don't want to hold our nose and vote for her, so we're going to vote for you. Please stay in the race,'" he said.

The campaigns of Colom and Hyde-Smith did not immediately respond to CNBC's requests for comment.

#3004. The midterm election could reshape the muni bond market. Here’s what investors need to know

The outcome of Tuesday's election is expected to have broad implications across the market, including municipal bonds.

The spotlight has been on the battle for congressional control and whether Democrats will wrest the House of Representatives, the Senate or both from Republicans. But for the muni market, what happens at the state level could have bigger repercussions — at least in the near term.

There is an array of initiatives on ballots around the nation that could change tax laws and add to bond supply. Taxes matter to muni investors since the income received is free of federal tax, as well as state tax if the holder lives in the state where the bond is issued.

Depending on the location, ballot measures are up for approval that would either provide tax breaks, or raise taxes on the wealthy.

"It's a complete bifurcation between red and blue states," said Dan Close, head of municipals at Nuveen. "Blue states are trying to put in place graduated income tax structures, trying to capture more revenue from high-income earners. Red states are promoting affordability by trying to cut property taxes."

Red states are those that predominantly vote Republican, while blue states swing toward Democrats.

For instance, California has a proposition known as the "billionaire tax," which would impose a one-time levy equal to 5% of the wealth of certain taxpayers with at least $1 billion in assets. The state also has a measure on the ballot that would make a temporary tax hike permanent on individuals earning over $371,000, adjusted annually for inflation. The tax is currently set to expire in 2031.

"The impact to the muni bond market will be to make the exemption more valuable," Close said of the possible passage of tax increases. Investors will likely seek shelter in the muni market, which would send bond prices higher, he noted.

It is a different story in Florida, where voters are set to decide whether to increase the Sunshine State's homestead exemption for non-school district property taxes. Qualified homeowners currently have a $25,000 property tax exemption, as well as another for $25,000 that doesn't apply to school property taxes. The new proposal raises the latter exemption to $150,000 in 2027 and $250,000 by 2028. New residents have a five-year waiting period before receiving the benefit.

If passed, some local governments will have to find other revenue sources or increase the overall tax rate to replace what they've lost, said Tom Kozlik, head of public policy and municipal strategy at HilltopSecurities.

"It's not necessarily going to result in downgrades for sure, but there would have to be some balancing with regard to overall revenues," he said.

Not all municipalities will be hurt equally, which means credit research is important, said Close.

"Like everything in municipal finance, it is really on a case-by-case basis that you could have some credit deterioration from [a] rollback of property taxes," he said.

Other voters on Tuesday will be asked to approve more state or local government debt. That could weigh on a market that has already seen heightened supply.

Some $473.8 billion has already been issued year to date, through September, according to the Securities Industry and Financial Markets Association. That's a 7.8% increase over the same period a year ago. In 2025, total muni issuance reached $587.3 billion, 14.3% higher than 2024, Sifma said.

Cooper Howard, director of fixed income research and strategy at the Schwab Center for Financial Research, believes supply is the biggest issue coming out of the election.

"That amount of issuance has been one of the reasons why you have to see that strong amount of demand continue to keep pace with it for total returns to keep up," Howard said.

Fortunately, new supply doesn't necessarily come quickly into the market. "There's a window over a couple years that they can issue," he noted.

The ultimate makeup of the next Congress could also have consequences for muni investors as lawmakers sort out their priorities. Most notable is what lawmakers will do to tackle the country's affordability problem, Kozlik at Hilltop said.

"There could be some tax policy initiatives and even affordability-related initiatives that manifest themselves in the first quarter of next year," he said. "That could impact housing. It could impact higher education. It could impact public power financing."

Investors considering moves before the election may want to look at those states with tax increases on the ballot, said Close at Nuveen.

For instance, Nuveen recently began offering a separately managed account dedicated to Washington after getting an enormous number of inquiries from financial advisors. The state recently enacted a 9.9% tax on individuals with taxable annual income above $1 million, set to go into effect in 2028. But the issue is now being put to voters, who can decide whether to repeal the new law.

"At the margin, if you do think that some of these measures are going to pass, knowing that bonds usually move up with an increase in taxes, there might be some opportunities to buy ahead of the election for blue states," Close said.

Howard believes munis are attractive right now, particularly for those who are in a tax bracket of 24% or above. Fundamentals are good and the bonds largely have good credit quality, he said. "About 70% of the broad muni index is either triple-A or double-A rated."

Kozlik is also bullish, calling yields "really attractive." He suggests investors concentrate on AA-rated or higher bonds.

"The muni space is part of the portfolio that most investors want," he said. "It helps them sleep well at night."

#3003. Inside the card-grading empire shaping the price of Pokémon cards

Collectors may have more grading options than ever, but many still turn to Professional Sports Authenticator, or PSA — even as the industry leader works through a backlog of millions of cards.

Demand for grading — an act of verifying a card’s authenticity and condition — has grown as collectors increasingly treat trading and vintage sports cards as alternative investments.

PSA reported a backlog of 7.2 million items on Oct. 20, down from 12.4 million in July. The company suspended cheaper submission services in June to ease the backlog before reopening its Value service on Oct. 29.

Even so, some sellers would rather wait for a PSA label than send their cards to a competitor, dealers told CNBC.

“It really all comes down to secondary market values,” said Geoff Wilson, founder and CEO of CardsHQ, which operates one of the world's largest sports card shops.

PSA-graded cards typically command higher prices than equivalent grades from competitors, Wilson said, giving collectors and investors an incentive to choose PSA in hopes of a stronger return.

The financial difference can be substantial.

A 1999 Base Set Charizard graded PSA 9 sold for about $3,440 on eBay on Sept. 29, compared with roughly $1,890 for a CGC 9 copy sold the same day, according to CNBC's review of PriceCharting records.

That pricing gap reinforces PSA's lead and gives its decisions about fees and submission services considerable influence over the resale market.

PSA had not responded to CNBC’s detailed questions at the time of publication. In published updates, the company has attributed service restrictions and pricing changes to demand exceeding capacity, and outlined investments intended to reduce the backlog.

Grading companies verify a card’s authenticity, assess its condition and seal it in a protective plastic case, known as a slab. PSA uses a 10-point scale, with its highest grade, Gem Mint 10, reserved for cards it describes as "virtually perfect."

Like a credit rating in bond markets, a card grade gives buyers and sellers a common benchmark for assessing condition.

That can reduce friction in transactions by giving both sides a reference point based on past sales of the same card at the same grade.

That capability has helped open the trading-card market to more global participation and made cross-border transactions easier, according to an unnamed spokesperson for Singapore-based submission dealer Oxley Grading.

The spokesperson attributed PSA’s position as the industry’s “gold standard” to its early start, brand recognition and extensive history of completed sales.

“They've just built up such a strong branding and a lot of sales records,” the spokesperson said.

They estimated that roughly 90% of the graded cards offered to Oxley Grading for purchase carry PSA’s label.

The dominance creates a self-reinforcing cycle: buyers seek out PSA-graded cards, giving sellers an incentive to use the company. The abundance of PSA slabs in shops and online marketplaces further reinforces the brand's visibility and demand.

For a seller choosing a grader, that established resale demand can outweigh a competitor’s lower fees or faster turnaround times.

PSA sits within a larger collectibles business.

Its parent company, Collectors, also owns rival grading brands Beckett and SGC, pricing-data platform Card Ladder, the PSA Vault and financial services. The vault links storage with selling through external marketplaces, including eBay.

Together, those businesses span much of a card’s commercial journey — grading, pricing, storage, sales and even financing against collectibles.

Collectors' three card-grading brands accounted for roughly 71% of August grading volume among the major firms tracked by industry analyst GemRate. PSA alone represented about 65%, while the next-largest player, CGC, represented about 28%.

Collectors' consolidation has also drawn legal scrutiny.

A proposed class action filed in April challenges Collectors’ acquisitions of SGC and Beckett, alleging that the deals unlawfully reduced competition in card grading. The allegations have not been proven in court.

Collectors has defended the Beckett acquisition as a way to strengthen a trusted brand while preserving collectors’ ability to choose the grading service that best suits their items.

“We believe that strong, well-supported brands help grow access to the hobby,” Collectors CEO Nat Turner said in the company's 2025 acquisition announcement.

For sellers who prioritize PSA's resale premium, changes to its fees and submission services can alter the economics of grading a card.

PSA raised fees across several existing tiers in February, including Value Bulk from $21.99 to $24.99 and Value from $27.99 to $32.99. It also lengthened estimated turnaround times for some services, saying the changes would help balance submission demand with grading capacity.

The suspension of cheaper tiers in June further narrowed affordable options, while higher costs have made customers more selective about which cards they submit, according to Oxley Grading's spokesperson.

Meanwhile, Wilson said the restrictions have particularly affected collectors and dealers who buy cards ungraded, send them to PSA and then resell them at a higher price.

For cards worth a few hundred dollars or less, that strategy has become harder to justify, he said.

“You're seeing more raw cards bought and sold at card shows,” Wilson added, using the industry term for ungraded cards.

PSA has acknowledged that customers are waiting too long.

In an Oct. 28 update, the company said submissions in April and May were 2.5 times the volume received in the same months a year earlier. It also announced shorter turnaround estimates for Standard and Priority services, while saying new Value submissions would be processed behind existing orders.

“We have added an incredible amount of capacity, but we still have work to do,” Ryan Hoge, president of PSA and grading at Collectors, said in the statement.

For Wilson, those investments in capacity are part of PSA's appeal.

“They've made the collector experience better by doing that,” he said.

The leading graders' dominance may leave collectors with little room to push back.

Collectors' preference for PSA's resale value can make switching graders less attractive, the Oxley Grading's spokesperson said, adding, "PSA's lead comes from resale demand and liquidity, and other labels are still building that."

Overseas customers can face fewer practical grading options as many major graders are based in the U.S., Amber Chuah, operations manager of trading-card platform TCG Marketplace, told CNBC.

The complaints she hears most often involve long waits and disappointment when cards receive lower grades than customers expected.

Challenging a grade can require another round of shipping and waiting, discouraging some collectors from disputing the result, she added.

"If you're really keen on selling, that's a lot of time."

PSA did not respond to CNBC's questions about the sources' claims.

PSA’s terms allow customers to request a paid review of disputed grades, with customers responsible for grading and applicable shipping fees. The company also offers defect notes for eligible submissions to provide more detail on how a grade was determined.

CardsHQ's Wilson said some variation between assessments is an inherent challenge in a process that involves human judgment.

Wilson pushes back against the suggestion that PSA’s position leaves customers without alternatives.

“First of all, there are alternatives,” he said, pointing to CGC, Beckett and smaller grading firms such as TAG.

CGC’s grading volume in August was more than double its level a year earlier, according to GemRate, showing that rival graders are attracting business. Beckett also recorded strong growth, although it is owned by Collectors.

Oxley Grading's spokesperson said there may be room for lower-cost services aimed at collectors who care more about a card’s artwork, sentimental value or preservation than maximizing its resale value.

Competing for sellers focused on the strongest financial return remains more difficult.

Building comparable resale acceptance would require substantial investment, with no guarantee that buyers would ultimately value a newcomer's label as highly as PSA's, the spokesperson said.

For Wilson, broader acceptance in the resale market is key to shifting that balance.

“If the market as a whole starts to respect the value of the other grading companies' cards more, then collectors will increasingly submit to those companies,” he said.

#3002. Takeaways from a Celtics 3-point blitz in a buzzy win over the Hornets

Maybe it was because it was an NBA Cup game. Maybe it was because Jayson Tatum was visiting his old stomping grounds. Maybe it was because the Celtics caught fire from 3. Mix that all together with a few notable absences from the Hornets roster and we got a 119-80 Boston blowout at The Spectrum Center on Friday Night.

Before being drafted in 2023, Hornets' Brandon Miller called Paul George his GOAT. You can certainly see the similarities. The lanky 6'9 swingmen are both three level scorers with the biggest difference being the decade-plus between them.

Unfortunately, Miller left the game in the second half with a groin soreness with 8 points, a rebound, assist, steal, and block.

His idol put up similar numbers. Miller hit just 3-of-10; George was worse at 3-of-11.

However, the mutual admiration society doesn't end there. Tatum once posted back in 2015 when he was at Chaminade that he was "Baby PG" and even boasted that he thinks that he was the first college player to wear the PG1s at Duke.

And whenever the Celtics travel down to North Carolina, it's always going to be a special trip for Tatum. Even though The Hive is 150 miles away from Cameron Indoor Stadium, Tatum just has superpowers playing in Charlotte. Out of the 29 other NBA teams, Tatum averages the most points against the Hornets at nearly 30 per game. And in an NBA Cup game where point differential matters, the defending Player of the Week scored 34 points in just 29 minutes. He was 11-for-11 from the free throw line and 5-of-8 from behind the arc and neared triple-double territory with eight rebounds and nine assists without playing a minute in the fourth quarter.

The Celtics flirted with the NBA record for most threes in a game at 29, a feat they tied on Opening Night in 2024 against the Knicks. Boston had 17 at halftime and had it not been a blowout, they might have hit at least five more.

Instead, they settled for the NBA Cup record at 24 (on 44 attempts).

Up the eastern seaboard, the Maine Celtics opened their G League season against the Westchester Knicks in blowout fashion as well 123-94. Rookie Dillon Mitchell had six dunks and a triple, including this alley-oop from Milos Uzan.

Uzan and Chris Cenac Jr. had 21 a piece in the win.

"Just trying to channel my anger into love." — head coach Joe Mazzulla to CelticsBlog's Noa Dalzell on his mid-game hug with Neemias Queta

Mazzulla called a timeout because Queta fell down (again) and instead of just yelling at him, he gave him a hug.

After two games, the Celtics are 2-0 with a +53 differential. After the Hawks beat the Wizards 139-132 on Friday night, Boston stands alone at the top of the Eastern Conference's Group C. They'll continue this leg of their road-heavy November with a stop in Milwaukee on Sunday and then the marquee face-melter on Tuesday against the 76ers.

#2997. Trump says Stephen Miller to replace Susie Wiles as White House chief of staff

President Donald Trump on Friday announced White House deputy chief of staff Stephen Miller will be promoted to chief of staff, replacing Susie Wiles.

Trump revealed the stunning shake-up just three days after Democrats won a landslide victory in the midterm elections, gaining majority control in the House and Senate and scrambling the trajectory of Trump's last two years in office.

Elevating Miller, an architect of Trump's controversial mass-deportation efforts, is bound to raise the hackles of the incoming class of congressional Democrats, who have already vowed to investigate the government's immigration policies.

"It has been the privilege of my lifetime to have served on his team from Day One, and I am honored beyond words to serve as his White House Chief of Staff," Miller said on X later Friday.

"The mission now is more clear than ever: Fight with all of our soul to support our President and defend this magnificent Republic," he said.

Miller will take over starting Monday, Trump said in a Truth Social post, praising his longtime aide as "one of my most trusted advisors."

Wiles, "who has done an outstanding job, will be moving over to the Trump Political Operation," the president wrote.

Asked about the move later Friday afternoon, Trump said, "I wanted to make a change."

"I think Susie's done a very good job, great job. But I just wanted to make it," Trump told reporters as he left the White House for an event in Cape Canaveral, Florida.

"Stephen's been great," Trump said, adding, "Sometimes in life you have to make moves."

Wiles resigned Thursday night, a senior White House source told MS NOW. Wiles told Trump, who was said to be angry with her following the GOP's huge election losses, "let me make this easy on you — I am going to resign," MS NOW reported.

"Today is my last day as White House Chief of Staff," Wiles said in an X post Friday afternoon. "Serving President Trump in the White House has been the honor of a lifetime."

"While my role may be changing, my commitment to the President and the mission never will," she wrote.

Senior administration officials were quick to congratulate Miller.

"Patriots in control," read an X post from Defense Secretary Pete Hegseth, whose job security has come into question after some Republicans, including Vice President JD Vance, blamed the midterm results on the Iran war.

Vance also praised Miller on X, saying he "will do great things."

The official X account for Trump's political operation celebrated the announcement by posting a black-and-white photo of Miller under the caption, "It's Miller time."

Democrats and other critics, meanwhile, filed in to denounce the pick.

House Minority Leader Hakeem Jeffries, D-N.Y., who could be elected House speaker next year, called Miller "a dangerous xenophobe and reckless extremist who has American blood on his hands."

"His presence in government should be eradicated like a malignant tumor. Fire him today," Jeffries wrote on X.

Senate Minority Leader Chuck Schumer, D-N.Y., wrote that Trump "learned nothing from his historic defeat."

"Stephen Miller is the architect of the most barbaric, inhumane policies of this administration. Trump couldn't have made a worse choice. Our Democratic majorities will fight him every step of the way," Schumer wrote.

Others took Miller's promotion as a sign that Trump, who seemed to express some willingness to negotiate with a blue Congress, will instead remain combative.

"For those who hoped the @POTUS might pivot to work with the incoming Congress, the appointment of Miller is a bucket of ICE cold water," wrote David Axelrod, a former senior advisor to President Barack Obama.

The White House reshuffle follows multiple resignations of senior staff in the Department of Justice since Election Day. They include Harmeet Dhillon, a leader in the DOJ's civil rights division, John Eisenberg, who headed the national security division, and Jason Reding Quiñones, who had been in charge of conspiracy investigations related to Trump's foes.

Wiles oversaw Trump's winning 2024 presidential campaign and has wielded major influence in his second administration, while largely staying out of the spotlight. She drew focus in late 2025, when Vanity Fair reported that Wiles admitted that Trump had pursued "retribution" as president and that he had an "alcoholic's personality."

Trump, and many other top administration officials, defended Wiles at the time, underscoring her status in the White House.

While Wiles is not fully leaving Trump's orbit, her resignation follows a pattern of other top female officials who have left or been fired from Trump's second administration, and then replaced by men.

#2995. Game Preview: Knicks at Cavaliers, November 7, 2026

Some members from Generation Z might believe that the history of the Cleveland Cavaliers started and ended with a man named LeBron James (not once but twice). They couldn't be more wrong. The Cavs joined the NBA in 1970 and accumulated plenty of interesting anecdotes through the years, especially early on. For example, they scouted the players for their expansion Draft with the help of trading cards that assistant coach Jim Lessig discovered while shopping for a few gallons of milk.

On that understandably subpar inaugural roster was also a man named Gary Suiter, whose biggest contributions were eating hot dogs in the stands during warmups and calling the front office disguised as a fan, lobbying for more playing time for—you guessed it—Gary Suiter. When the well-known bachelor tried to shake down the Cavs for bigger travel expenses by hiring a prostitute to play his wife, even the desperate Cavaliers had enough and kicked him out.

Later on, though, basketball really took off in Ohio. The Cavs featured some really exciting teams, especially when the aforementioned James came to town and then came again to actually win a title. Other teams that are fondly remembered in "The Land" are the squads from the late 1980s and 1990s that featured stalwarts like Mark Price, Ron Harper, Brad Daugherty, or Craig Ehlo. Exactly: the guy who is mostly remembered for crumbling to the floor after Michael Jordan's famous first "shot", one of the most iconic images in league history.

How history and Cleveland will remember the current iteration of Cavaliers remains to be seen, yet it might very well be decided this year. The headliners on Kenny Atkinson's team are mostly unchanged from last year's Eastern Conference Finals sweep at the hands our Knickerbockers. That result could be described as another conquered mountain ledge on the Knicks' way to the NBA summit, or seen as just another failure by a Cavs' core that's been trying to win it all for about three years now.

At the heart of that core has been Donovan Mitchell and he is still the headliner at Rocket Arena this season. The stocky highflyer is scoring an efficient 22.5 points per game while probably sporting one of the more explosive games in the league these days. Fans are lamenting his decision making though, criticizing his constant hero ball and what they get for a massive $273 million dollar deal he signed in the summer. Next to Mitchell, a now 37-year-old James Harden runs the point for the Cavaliers, which might also impact "Spida" negatively. The former MVP is showing his age early on, only connects on 38 percent of his shots, and looks disinterested doing it every second night out.

Evan Mobley and Jarrett Allen still form Cleveland's frontline—with all their shortcomings, yet also with plenty of talent. The ultra versatile Mobley has taken his physique and his game to another level in recent years, sports a much better jumper from the outside, and the former Defensive Player of the Year isn't moved off his spots as easily he used to be. If you want a big to switch effortlessly on Jalen Brunson, he's your man. His current season has been particularly promising with shooting splits of 56/48.6 (we'll ignore the 63 percent from the line this time around). Allen is kind of there: big, athletic, and skilled, yet you won't see him in any Strongman or Tough Mudder competitions any time soon.

Even if Cleveland got a bit longer and a bit more athletic, you still have to wonder where all of this is really going. They are good but how good really? Can they really compete with the top teams in the East? So far there is just a lot of "meh" in their profile. Standing at 5-4 they rank 9th in offensive rating and 14th in defensive rating. They play with a solid yet unspectacular pace (thank you, James Harden) and are average at best on the boards or from the field. Cleveland doesn't turn the ball over, which is good, yet doesn't force any turnovers either, which is bad (only the Bucks and Knicks make the opponent cough the ball up fewer times than Cleveland).

To be fair, Cleveland is a team that for the most part knows who it is, and such groups can take a little time shaking off the rust, especially after a long last season—we as Bocker buddies know all about it. The Cavaliers, just like the Knicks again, have a lot of things going for them, but it seems like Cleveland unlike New York also has a definitive ceiling. I'd mostly call it James Harden as he has never shown that he can be the ultimate difference maker in the postseason, at least not in a good way. Until then of course the Cavaliers are a perfectly fine, quality opponent, especially at home. They just proved it in their NBA cup last night against the Philadelphia 76ers, dismantling the fighting Nurses 119-95.

Let's hope our Knicks fare a little better tonight and that the biggest historical significance of this game is another win for the good guys. At the very least we don't have to worry about any of our players binge eating in the stands before the game, I guess. As funny as that would be.

Who: New York Knicks (4-4) at Cleveland Cavaliers (5-4)Date: Saturday, November 7, 2026Time: 8:00 PM ETPlace: Rocket Arena, Cleveland, OHTV: MSG, NBATVFollow: @ptknicksblog and bsky

→ Baca artikel asli

#2993. World’s No. 1 restaurant crowned as contest comes under increasing scrutiny

Asador Etxebarri in Atxondo, Spain, is the world's top restaurant.

That's according to The World's 50 Best Restaurants, which announced results on Wednesday evening in Lima, Peru. Asador Etxebarri, helmed by chef Bittor Arguinzoniz, sits in Basque Country and features grilled items like a charred beef chop, fresh mozzarella from the chef's buffalo, or house-made chorizo.

Chef Gaggan Anand's Gaggan in Bangkok, which combines progressive Indian cuisine with French, Thai and Japanese influences, was No. 2, and Alchemist in Copenhagen, from head chef Rasmus Munk, took the third spot.

India returned to the top 50 for the first time since 2004, with Masque at No. 26 and NAAR at No. 49. Mainland China made the list for the first time since 2021, with Meet the Bund in Shanghai at No. 21 and Ru Yuan in Hangzhou at No. 36.

Chef-restaurateur Ferran Adrià, who achieved worldwide renown with his avant-garde techniques at his now-closed restaurant El Bulli, received the SevenRooms Icon Award. Adria continues to support culinary research and education through the nonprofit El Bulli Foundation.

The 50, as the umbrella organization is called, creates lists of restaurants, bars, hotels and vineyards that it ranks as top in the world or region. It draws on industry networks to help rank establishments.

For years, The 50 has been seen as a kingmaker and an opportunity for restaurants and bars worldwide. Being "on the list," many chefs and other restaurant-industry insiders have said, makes a huge difference for reservations and revenues.

But there's been increased scrutiny of the contest as well. Chef Ana Roš of Hiša Franko in Slovenia, which was ranked 60th this year, posted on Instagram that she wouldn't be attending the Lima ceremony, saying she would rather invest resources "in our guests, our team, our producers and the daily work of making the restaurant better."

While stressing that she had enjoyed a "vibrant community" at the 50 Best and made many friends from it, she said "over time, however, success within the ranking has come to depend increasingly on visibility within the system itself," and that promotion of one's restaurant risked becoming as important as the work happening inside it.

She appears not to have been alone. Many of the world's top chefs consider awards and lists to be a tricky balance with their work at their establishment.

"Guides and lists now shape the industry they claim to assess," Ivan Brehm, chef-owner of Michelin-starred Nouri in Singapore, told CNBC. "Their judgments influence where diners spend money, where investment flows, which chefs get attention and what younger cooks learn to aspire to."

He added of the 50 that "none of this denies the brilliance of the restaurants on the list or the opportunities it has created for all of us. The question is what it sidelines and what it incentivizes."

Roš' Instagram post had more than 10,400 likes as of Friday, including from top chefs worldwide.

"The reaction and the reach were unexpected" from the post, Roš told CNBC in an email. "I did not write a post to provoke, but more as an eye-opener. And apparently a lot of people have the same perception as I do."

The 50 defended its list and role within the industry.

"The 50 does not claim to have the capabilities to police establishments on their budgets or surrounding activities," a spokesperson told CNBC. "The 50's role is to provide a snapshot of expert opinions on the best hospitality experiences in a given region or category. It does not act as a regulatory body and it applies the same voting rules and principles consistently across all countries and regions."

The time and effort put into the contest can be a dilemma, too.

"We didn't go to Lima this year. It's a huge amount of time in the air for a very short amount of time on the ground and, like Ana, I felt my time was better spent being present in our restaurant with our team and our guests," said Dave Pynt, chef-owner of Michelin-starred Burnt Ends in Singapore, who won the 50 Best Chef's Choice Award in 2017.

However, he added that "when the awards are closer and easier to get to, I love going. They're fun, you learn a lot, and you get to catch up with mates and people in the industry you don't get to see enough."

The Federal Reserve Board on Friday released the results of the 2025 Survey of Consumer Finances, which provides the public and policymakers with detailed insights into the economic condition of American families.

The data provide a representative picture of what Americans own, how and how much they borrow, and how they bank. The current version of the survey has been undertaken every three years since 1989 and provides the public with an extensive time series of thorough information on the economic conditions of American families. Alongside the survey data, the Board issued its summary report entitled Changes in U.S. Family Finances from 2022 to 2025 (PDF), which examines changes in income, net worth, assets, debt, and financial vulnerability.

The survey is conducted for the Board by NORC, a social science research organization at the University of Chicago. Participants in the study are chosen at random from 119 geographic areas, including metropolitan areas and rural counties across the United States, using scientific sampling procedures that aim to produce a sample representative of American families.

#2988. Democrat shaping AI policy bought chip stocks despite backing congressional trading ban

Rep. Josh Gottheimer, a Democrat helping write his party's artificial intelligence agenda, has disclosed new purchases of AI-linked stocks — just months after voting to ban lawmakers and their families from buying individual shares.

The New Jersey Democrat disclosed an Oct. 12 purchase of between $1,001 and $15,000 of Benchmark Electronics stock, according to a filing released this week. His jointly owned Morgan Stanley account also bought up to $15,000 worth of Applied Materials shares on Oct. 26, following three earlier purchases of leading AI chipmaker Advanced Micro Devices.

While there's no evidence of wrongdoing, the trades put Gottheimer's investments in the spotlight as Democrats prepare to take control of Congress in January, gaining new power to regulate and investigate the AI industry.

Gottheimer, a former Microsoft executive, sits on the House Financial Services subcommittee overseeing AI in finance, has introduced legislation on AI regulations and co-chairs a commission developing Democrats' broader AI agenda.

Gottheimer's spokesman, Haraden Bottomley, said the congressman turned over control of his investments to a third party with "full investment discretion" before taking office in 2017.

"Gottheimer is also awaiting approval from Congress of a blind trust. In the meantime, he has taken the extra step of setting up an independent trust," Bottomley said.

Democrats are already targeting the AI industry. Reps. Maxine Waters and Greg Casar introduced a resolution opposing taxpayer bailouts for AI companies, referred in part to Gottheimer's committee. Casar has also called for AI executives to testify under oath, while other Democrats are pushing to restrict data centers or halt new construction altogether.

The purchases also contrast with Gottheimer's longstanding support for restricting congressional stock trading. In July, he was among just 13 Democrats who voted for the Stop Insider Trading Act, which would prohibit lawmakers and their families from purchasing most individual stocks. The bill passed the House but has not become law. Some Democrats opposed it both because it contained a voter-ID provision and because some said it should have gone further in curbing stock purchasing and holding by elected officials.

Four years earlier, Gottheimer backed legislation requiring lawmakers to place investments in blind trusts and pledged to establish one himself.

The AI purchases are just a small slice of Gottheimer's sprawling investment portfolio. The five trades totaled between $5,005 and $75,000, compared with $15.1 million to $59 million in disclosed household assets, including up to $44.5 million in stocks. His household has reported more than 1,100 securities transactions over three years, according to Follow the Filings. Gottheimer reports household assets jointly with his wife, Marla Tusk, who is general counsel at a political consulting firm.

Advanced Micro Devices competes with Nvidia in AI chips, while Applied Materials supplies semiconductor manufacturing equipment. It was also Benchmark's largest disclosed customer in 2025, accounting for 14% of revenue, according to a securities filing.

Benchmark, meanwhile, generated 30% of its third-quarter revenue from semiconductor equipment and another 16% from advanced computing and communications, according to its Oct. 28 earnings report. Gottheimer also disclosed an Applied Materials purchase in August and a sale in September.

The purchases came shortly before a sharp selloff in AI stocks. The Nasdaq Composite Index plunged 5.5% Thursday, as Nvidia dropped more than 9% and AMD roughly 17%. The index includes both companies.

Benchmark fell nearly 17% over Wednesday and Thursday, closing at $59.34 — roughly 25% below its closing price on Gottheimer's October purchase date — before recovering somewhat Friday.

#2986. Bucks vs. Raptors Player Grades: Burries shows out, Turner no-shows in second-half collapse

In their first NBA Cup game, the Milwaukee Bucks followed a terrific first half with a disappointing second half and lost to the wounded Toronto Raptors, 121-107. The Bucks led most of the game and at one point were up 15; their first-half defence was great, which let them get out and score easily in transition. However, the second half, in which the Raptors scored 70 points, was what got the home side. Now, it doesn't take much for me to get invested in the Bucks—whatever stage they're at—but for whatever reason, this loss made me feel pretty damn bleak. Against a team without most of its primary offensive engines, Milwaukee should have been able to pull it out, and losing was really disheartening. Anyway, read our full summary of the game here and catch a six-minute audio recap on the Bucks+ podcast, Bucks In Six Minutes, below.

14 minutes, 10 points, 1 rebound, 1 assist, 2 steals, 5 turnovers, 3/3 FG, 1/1 3P, -12

Annnnnd we're back to the bad version of Rollins. The issue in this one was obviously his turnovers; the Raptors like to apply heaps of pressure to the ball handler, so you have to stay composed with the ball. Rollins was rushed and, at times, just plain nonchalant; I mentioned in the Rapid Recap one instance in the fourth where he threw a left-handed whip pass across the middle of the floor that ended up in a layup down the other end. The other problem was Rollins' defence. There were a few instances where his positioning was off, and he allowed his man to reject the screen and knife into the lane; Jenkins threw his hands up in disgust on one of them.

35 minutes, 23 points, 5 rebounds, 10 assists, 3 turnovers, 7/16 FG, 1/5 3P, 0

Herro's best playmaking game as a Buck, with five assists in the second quarter alone. The team had much more direction and flow when Herro was on the floor—evidenced by him being one of just two Bucks without a negative plus/minus—which they completely lost when he was off the floor.

27 minutes, 14 points, 3 rebounds, 3 assists, 1 block, 4/10 FG, 2/7 3P , 4/4 FT, -6

Jaquez started like a house on fire, scoring half of his points in the first few minutes of the game. I liked his use of "stampede cuts" in this one as well, where the player starts moving to the rim before the ball reaches their hands, catching their defender off guard. Jaquez had a few nice instances using stampedes to get into the lane and create. Unfortunately, his three-point shooting failed him for the most part.

32 minutes, 13 points, 4 rebounds, 3 assists, 2 steals, 3 turnovers, 6/9 FG, 1/4 3P, +3

Overall, Kuzma played a solid game and, in particular, a great first half; he scored all 13 of his points on 5/6 shooting in the first, which included some very instinctive tip-ins. I liked how active he was as a screener, sprinting into the screen and getting himself in the right spots to receive the ball and finish around the rim. Additionally, Kuzma was basically their only credible defender for RJ Barrett, and for the most part, he did a good job with that assignment. BUT, as I seem to say every game, Kuzma did some enraging stuff too. He did his nightly drive to the rim with no vision for what else was going on, and most of his turnovers seemed to be live-ball. Moreover, I've noticed a trend where he makes a mistake and then gets pissy at his teammates for some reason, as if it were their fault.

23 minutes, 0 points, 7 rebounds, 1 steal, 2 blocks, 2 turnovers, 0/7 FG, 0/4 3P, -4

Good golly. I don't know, man, sometimes I want to shake Turner, but as Zac replied to me, "He'd probably break; his hands seem to shatter on every rebound or catch attempt." Like, why is it so hard for a seven-footer like him to play more physically? The Raptors made Turner look like a child last night, which I was hoping wouldn't happen because Toronto was meant to be one of the trade suitors for him! Myles, this was your audition. SMH.

13 minutes, 4 points, 3 rebounds, 1 block, 2/7 FG, 0/4 3P, 0

Another rough game for Ware, who seemingly can't hit a three in a Bucks uniform, down to 20% on the year. Also not great to see him throw a fit on the sidelines after getting subbed off.

10 minutes, 3 points, 2 assists, 1 steal, 1 turnover, 1/2 FG, 0/1 3P, -9

Kind of a nothing-y game from Jakucionis. Again, he didn't do much wrong, but his niche of finding angles and getting into the lane looked harder against a gritty squad like the Raptors. KJ did make a great cut in the first half for his only field goal, though, which I loved.

38 minutes, 21 points, 11 rebounds, 6 assists, 1 block, 1 turnover, 8/14 FG, 1/5 3P, 4/6 FT, -5

Burries had his "I've arrived" game. I mean, we can talk about the scoring and athleticism around the rim, but I'm more interested in how he seems to grab every rebound in sight. It's surreal to watch. Grabbing six boards in the first quarter, Burries had this "I'm not losing tonight, so jump on my back" vibe about him. The kid just has this nose for the ball and a will to pursue it like very few players I've seen. Burries is showing he's a winner, and I can't wait for him to gain confidence from this game and give us more like it in the future.

24 minutes, 12 points, 1 block, 2 turnovers, 4/9 FG, 4/8 3P, -19

Whenever the Raptors looked like they were about to get the game out of reach, Green would hit a three. One thing I will always appreciate about Green is that, despite his unassuming nature, he is never scared of the moment. Hit two huge ones in the fourth.

15 minutes, 7 points, 1 block, 3/5 FG, -11

Ament got two of his three buckets in transition, while the other came on a nice baseline cut where he smartly waited for the defender to jump, then calmly put in the and-one. Not many other notes, other than that I thought Ament's minutes were generally quite positive.

Sims was forced into extended minutes because Turner had early foul trouble and Ware looked borderline unplayable at times. He did his job defensively, but you could tell the Raptors were happy to double all on-ball screens Sims set because they didn't trust his decision-making out of the short roll, which is a strategy we saw from opponents last year too.

Based on Jenkins' postgame comments, the team clearly wasn't doing much of what they had been instructed to do in practice. His decision to close with no centre was the right one, but that then made it tough to rebound. Part of the issue is that the Bucks are leaning on flawed veterans; you don't want to turn to Kuzma to close as a two-way wing, but there aren't many other great options, especially when you needed someone beefy enough to deal with Barrett. Jaquez is a fine player, but he's a seventh man on a good team. Turner is a great locker room guy, but he drives you mad with his lack of physicality. I say this to reiterate that Jenkins is working with a rough set of cards right now, and I struggle to think another coach could have got a different result with this same group of personnel.

DNP-CD: Ousmane Dieng, Caris LeVert, Gary Trent Jr.

Inactive: Kam Jones, Cormac Ryan, Pete Nance, Bogoljub Markovic,

Milwaukee's centre rotation might be the league's worst when both play poorly.

Jenkins was hot in his postgame presser, cutting off a reporter's question about what he saw from his struggling offence in the second half: "We were getting stops in the first half. We were playing fast. We didn't have to call sets. But then we just gave up second-chance opportunities, fouled them way too much, and they were taking the ball out of the net, so we couldn't play as fast as we needed to."

Jenkins also mentioned that the Bucks didn't "tag up," which means that when the shot goes up, all five players get to their man, which, when done right, impedes the opponent's ability to advance the ball up the floor and score in transition.

Jenkins said that after the game, Turner owned his bad performance to the group.

The broadcast showed a stat that over the last three games, Herro had scored 0 points in the first quarter and 62 in all other quarters. Crazy.

Speaking of Herro, I noticed he subbed back in earlier than usual in the fourth, which resulted in him breaking the ~30-minute pseudo-limit he had seemingly been operating under previously.

#2985. Top Wall Street analysts are bullish on these 3 stocks for the long haul

The ongoing earnings season, Middle East tensions and buzz ahead of the U.S. midterm elections have been keeping investors busy. Further, worries about elevated artificial intelligence spending are weighing on investor sentiment.

Investors looking beyond the short-term noise and focusing on long-term returns can track top Wall Street analysts to pick the right stocks, backed by strong research.

Here are three stocks favored by some of Wall Street's top pros, according to TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

This week's first stock pick is Datadog, an AI-powered observability and security platform. The company will announce its results for the third quarter on Nov. 4. Datadog is seeing strong demand for its offerings, thanks to cloud migration and rapid AI adoption. TipRanks' KPIs tool for DDOG shows the growth in its customer base over the past few years amid increasing market penetration and growing adoption of its products.

Heading into Q3 FY26 earnings, Bank of America analyst Koji Ikeda reiterated a buy rating on Datadog stock and raised his price target to $335 from $305. The analyst noted that industry checks and data indicate that infrastructure software demand is strengthening, with AI increasingly driving usage and growth.

Ikeda called Datadog and Atlassian his top picks, saying that companies that can show that AI is a tailwind to growth could see their valuation rerate, even at premium multiples. He added that the total addressable market for observability is growing faster than anticipated, driven by accelerated growth of enterprise AI and the rapid ramp of consumer AI agents.

The five-star analyst believes that focus will be on revenue growth, which he estimates to be 28.7% for Q3 FY26 versus the Street's consensus of 29.1%. Ikeda thinks that the market is underestimating the growth prospects of DDOG's security products. Meanwhile, he cautioned investors about concentration risk related to large customers.

"We think results will be supportive of our long-term thesis that Datadog is a strong share gainer in the rapidly expanding observability opportunity," said Ikeda.

Ikeda ranks No. 673 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 55% of the time, delivering an average return of 10.9%. See Datadog Statistics on TipRanks.

The next pick, semiconductor giant Nvidia, is also benefiting from AI-driven demand for its products. After attending Nvidia's GTC Berlin conference, Morgan Stanley analyst Joseph Moore reaffirmed a buy rating on NVDA stock with a price target of $300, calling it a top pick in the semiconductor space.

The five-star analyst said that the conference supported his thesis that Nvidia continues to effectively navigate bottlenecks in the supply chain, land/power/shell, and funding. In fact, Moore is "convinced that all 3 aspects are opportunities for NVDA."

While Nvidia's GTC Berlin offered no major updates, Moore noted the conference reflected that the company continues to add to its significant advantages heading into 2027. The analyst said that Vera Rubin is gaining momentum, though details on next year's Rubin Ultra were lighter than expected.

With demand outpacing supply, Moore said that focus is on closing the gap. In this regard, he highlighted the supply chain deals on substrate, packaging and testing. While the analyst continues to view TSMC 3 nm wafer supply as the biggest challenge, he expects it to ease over time.

Moore ranks No. 142 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 59% of the time, delivering an average return of 23.4%. See Nvidia Ownership Structure on TipRanks.

Aerospace company SpaceX recently announced solid third-quarter results, with its AI revenue surging to $7.56 billion from $813 million in the prior-year quarter.

Impressed by the results, Needham analyst Ryan Koontz reiterated a buy rating on SPCX stock with a price target of $250. Koontz highlighted that SpaceX's Q3 results significantly exceeded expectations, proving that the company's vertically integrated business model is driving massive scale.

Furthermore, Koontz emphasized the 195.2% sequential growth in SpaceX's AI revenue, fueled by wholesale spot compute deals. He noted management's increasing focus on its AI strategy, given that it is optimistic about adding 10 gigawatts of incremental capacity during 2027.

"With increasing confidence on Starship re-use, we meaningfully increase our estimates," said Koontz.

Koontz ranks No. 357 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 42% of the time, delivering an average return of 23.7%. See SpaceX Technical Analysis on TipRanks.

#2984. Data center double-down: Hassett says blue wave won't stop AI build-out

National Economic Council Director Kevin Hassett said Friday he expects data center growth to continue as projected, despite the blue-wave election that saw voters overwhelmingly favor limiting the tech infrastructure buildout.

Hassett, on CNBC's "Squawk on the Street," acknowledged the dozens of measures on states' midterm ballots seeking to constrain data centers, a crucial part of President Donald Trump's push to win the global artificial intelligence race.

In at least 46 of the roughly 50 such initiatives on ballots in 2026, majorities voted to restrict development of the facilities, according to Ballotpedia's tally.

"We respect the localities that decide that's what they want to do," Hassett said. "But America is a big place, and right now, the data centers scheduled over the next couple of years aren't affected by these ballot initiatives."

"And the stuff after that, you know, there's plenty of room in the U.S. for data center growth to continue, as it was projected ahead of the election," he said.

Hassett said he expects data centers will continue to expand in states with more welcoming governors, and he suggested that even hostile states will eventually come around.

"That's exactly what's going to happen," he said. "That, or what'll happen will be that the benefits of the data centers will be so obvious that people decide to change their minds about the way they regulate them."

Hassett also blamed Thursday's tech-driven market plunge on "uncertainty about what the political change is going to mean for data centers going forward."

"We've now got a ... new majority that maybe is going to go in there and muddle around in there, and I think that markets are anxious about that," he said.

"But I think that the economic strength that we see everywhere else is so strong, and of course, in the end, you could take something that's growing as fast as the data centers and make it grow a little bit slower, and you still have a blockbuster economy."

"And so, I think we're still very, very optimistic about what's going to happen going forward," he said.

The CNBC Voter Poll found 56% of midterm voters identified data centers as either a crucial or major issue — and of those who considered it crucial, 62% favored the generic Democratic candidate.

Trump has strongly embraced AI and data centers and has even tried to reverse their seeming unpopularity by rebranding the tech as "super intelligence," or SI.

In a Truth Social post Friday, Trump made clear that despite the midterm losses, his support for the nascent industry has not faltered.

"As President, I am 100% in favor of Super Intelligence (SI), and will work with the many States in the U.S. that want to become RICH by having them," Trump wrote. "They are GOLD, and only foolish leadership will say 'NO' to the riches they bring!"

#2981. Microsoft plans to use fewer neoclouds as AI buildout shows signs of strain

Microsoft is shrinking its roster of neocloud partners as it focuses on work with a few key suppliers and builds out its own data centers, according to people familiar with the matter.

The software giant's plans pose another potential challenge for a corner of the artificial intelligence market that's showing signs of strain due to rising borrowing costs, hefty debt loads and concerns about whether the buildout will slow down.

Microsoft has forged relationships in recent years with a number of neoclouds, including CoreWeave, Nebius, Chronoscale, IREN, Lambda and Nscale, as well as with Oracle, which is pouring money into building out AI infrastructure.

Microsoft is now in the evaluation stage and hasn't decided which neoclouds it will stop using, said two sources, who asked not to be named because the talks are confidential. One of the people said Microsoft wants to prioritize its own data center footprint. Bloomberg reported on the plans earlier. Two people said that Microsoft opted not to renew a deal with Oracle.

A Microsoft spokesperson said in an emailed statement that its infrastructure strategy is "built on flexibility and choice."

"We combine Microsoft-owned datacenters, leased facilities and strategic partnerships with third-party providers to deliver the capacity our customers need, where and when they need it," the spokesperson said. "We regularly review these investments against customer demand. That includes tracking delivery and adjusting plans as circumstances change."

The neoclouds have been a turbulent trade on Wall Street in recent weeks as rising interest rates added to fears that many companies have become overly reliant on debt in rushing to build capacity. Shares of CoreWeave, Nebius and IREN are all down by roughly half over the past month even after rallying on Friday.

A recent industry review counted 323 neoclouds, and experts have predicted that many of them won't survive.

In its earnings report on Thursday, IREN said it handed over data center capacity to Microsoft in August, but Microsoft requested a networking equipment upgrade that will result in the infrastructure coming online in January or February instead of this year.

Microsoft finance chief Amy Hood said on the software company's fiscal first-quarter earnings call in October that some cloud contracts involving graphics processing units were delayed until the first half of 2027, affecting Azure cloud growth. Microsoft reported $45.2 billion worth of capital expenditures and finance leases during the latest quarter, and said capex spending will accelerate in the second half of the fiscal year.

CoreWeave and Nebius are both scheduled to report quarterly results next week. IREN shares popped 16% on Friday after the company said AI revenue more than doubled from the prior quarter to $200 million. IREN co-CEO Daniel Roberts told CNBC that there's a "disconnect between what we're seeing in public market and what we're seeing at the customer level."

However, he acknowledged the challenge that market conditions present.

"Capital markets have become more selective in the last few weeks, and the questions have undoubtedly moved from how big the contract is to whether it can be delivered, financed, and run at a profit," Roberts said on the earnings call.

Microsoft has already slashed its work with U.K.-based neocloud Nscale, walking away from a site that it had intended to use in England. Darren Hardman, Microsoft's CEO for the U.K and Ireland, told CNBC in October that the decision had to do with Nscale's ability to get access to electricity.

The Financial Times reported this week that Microsoft decided against renewing a contract with Nscale in Texas. Nscale filed to go public in mid-September but hasn't updated its prospectus since then. The company said in its IPO filing that it expects Microsoft to be a "significant customer in future periods."

Microsoft's big turn to neoclouds came after the launch of ChatGPT in 2022, which led to a surge in demand for GPUs. In looking elsewhere, Microsoft signed up CoreWeave, which was a privately held startup at the time with roots in cryptocurrency mining. When CoreWeave went public last year, it said 62% of its 2024 revenue came from Microsoft.

At the time of ChatGPT's release, OpenAI only used Azure. Now the company's cloud suppliers also include Amazon, Cerebras, CoreWeave, Google and Oracle.

Microsoft's spokesperson said that its approach to reviewing neocloud investments is "a normal and responsible way to manage large, complex infrastructure projects," and that "the pace of our new agreements reflects what we need today."

—CNBC's Kif Leswing contributed to this report.

#2978. Jayson Tatum admits Charlotte ‘feels like home’ after NBA Cup masterclass

Despite being hundreds of miles away from Boston, Jayson Tatum had no trouble feeling at ease in Charlotte, North Carolina, on Friday night. For a moment, the Spectrum Center even began to sound like TD Garden.

Chants of 'Let's go Celtics' began filling the arena. In response, the Hornets began turning up the volume on their signature buzzing sound effect in an attempt to drown out the Celtics fans taking over Charlotte. It didn't work. When Tatum arrived at the free-throw line in the third quarter, he was greeted by a chorus of loud 'MVP' chants.

"It definitely feels like home," Tatum confessed after Boston's 119-80 NBA Cup win, per CLNS Media.

Tatum found his footing and began painting his latest masterpiece early on. He scored or assisted on 30 of the Celtics' first 40 first-quarter points, which tied Paul Pierce for the most 15-point first quarters (106) in the franchise's play-by-play history, according to ESPN Insights. By halftime, he had reached 24 points on 8-of-14 shooting, including 5-of-8 from beyond the arc, surpassing his combined 3-point makes from his three previous games.

If he wanted to, Tatum could've pursued his career-high (60 points). But instead, his night was called off after three quarters as the Celtics held a 96-57 runaway lead.

At that point, the damage was already done. Tatum only needed three frames to finish with 34 points, eight rebounds, and nine assists, falling a hair shy of securing his first triple-double of the season. He earned a fourth quarter of rest, watching from the bench as head coach Joe Mazzulla allowed reserves including Luka Garza, Mike Conley, and Ron Harper Jr. to get their reps in until the final buzzer.

The Celtics found a way to turn a trip to Charlotte into a home-away-from-home experience. For Tatum, as uplifting as the Boston fans in attendance may have been, the 28-year-old had other reasons to feel comfortable in North Carolina, dating back to his teenage years.

"I have a lot of connections here in this state, this area," he explained. "I came here as an 18-year-old kid, my first time spending real time away from home. I was here for a little under a year, and obviously the connection I have here with Duke and the relationships that I've been able to build. I always love coming back to North Carolina and love playing here. A lot of Celtics fans, a lot of Duke fans. It's always a good time coming back."

Throughout his career, Tatum has always flipped the switch in Charlotte. He's averaged 29.9 points with eight rebounds and 3.7 assists across 15 career games — not including Friday nights — against the Hornets on the road.

CHARLOTTE, NC – NOVEMBER 6: Jayson Tatum #0 of the Boston Celtics shoots the ball during the game against the Charlotte Hornets during the 2026 Emirates NBA Cup on November 6, 2026 at Spectrum Center in Charlotte, North Carolina. For as many 'MVP' chants as Tatum has drawn and will continue to draw this season, this performance certainly lived up to the billing. He did everything. He entered the night shooting 41.8 percent from the field and 22.9 percent from three before going 56.3 percent and 62.5 percent, respectively, in Charlotte.

"It was a pretty damn good night," Payton Pritchard told reporters, per CLNS Media. "34 is pretty good."

Tatum unleashed his dominance, both as a scorer and as a playmaker. He was the undisputed leader, carrying out a belt-to-backside thrashing that took Hornets head coach Charles Lee's group out of contention well before the game had reached its end.

He also got to the line plenty, going a perfect 11-for-11 from the charity stripe.

Pritchard continued: "He plays the right way, and he makes great decisions and [he's] one heck of a ball player for sure."

In each of his first eight appearances this season, Tatum has recorded six-plus assists.

It's been only eight months since Tatum returned from his ruptured Achilles tendon last season, which is much more recent than it might sound. He's still working toward completing his first full season since returning, and as much as he's continued to pass the eye test, Tatum still doesn't consider himself 100 percent just yet.

"Hell no," Tatum told CelticsBlog's Noa Dalzell. "I wish. I'm getting there, but no. How I felt before I got hurt was really, really good. I don't feel too far, and again, I still only played less than 40 games since I've been back, so still in the quote-unquote rehab phase of returning to play."

Still, he isn't discouraged by the process.

"We're gonna get there, though," Tatum added.

On Wednesday, the Celtics survived letting go of a 22-point lead against the Milwaukee Bucks as Tatum rested on the sidelines. In Charlotte, they witnessed Tatum make the most of his additional rest and take another step toward becoming the leader Boston will need this season.

#2977. Why a wave of bomb threats is proving so difficult for Europe's aviation authorities to contain

A spate of bomb threats against passenger flights has forced diversions, emergency landings and military escorts across European airspace this week, stoking fear among passengers and heaping pressure on airlines.

Authorities have described the more than two dozen threats as malicious hoaxes, and no actual attacks have been carried out. But the incidents have highlighted the huge impact that even false claims can have on a tightly scheduled aviation network.

"This is really quite exceptional, I've never seen this before in my whole career," Wilfried Covent, head of security firm Covent Consulting with a specialization in aviation, told CNBC by phone.

"We've had bomb threats in flights and towards airports, but usually maybe a single copycat, one or two max. What we see now is a systematic attack on airlines and airports."

An email sent to European broadcasters on Friday claimed to be from the leader of the group behind the plane hoaxes and also said bombs had been placed in their buildings, prompting evacuations. No bombs were found at the sites.

"What happened this week is just a small part of what awaits Europe in the near future," the message said, according to Reuters.

The news agency said it could not verify the sender's identity, where the emails originated or whether they were linked to the threats against flights.

Covent said the "criminals" — who many security analysts say are likely to be state-backed actors working in coordination to disrupt public life for Europeans, but have not been identified — were well aware that the "weakest point of aviation is targeting it in-flight."

"With a threat on an airport or a general threat to aircraft, you can assess the situation without fully disrupting operations. When the threat is called on a specific flight number that is in the air, even with a hoax, that triggers international regulations which ensure you don't ignore it — it means a quick landing, evacuation and searches by specialist forces," he told CNBC.

"For the context of an assessment, you cannot say that 20 times before it has been a hoax so this time it is a hoax, the safety of passengers and crew is priority number one. The criminals know that."

Responsibility for dealing with hoax attacks is split, international agencies told CNBC. Assessing security threats and implementing aviation security measures primarily falls to national authorities, working in coordination with airport operators, airlines and law enforcement.

Air traffic control will also be involved in finding aircraft with an appropriate place to land, while the European Union Aviation Safety Agency, or EASA and U.K.'s Civil Aviation Authority more broadly set aviation safety rules and set how airlines prepare their crews to respond to security threats. Within the European Union, the European Commission is involved in aviation security policy and coordination between member states.

The operational decisions also depend on where the threat arises. Joji Waites, director of flight safety, policy and regulation at the British Air Line Pilots Association, said airline assessors grade a threat as red, amber or green, but that an amber verdict would never be given if the plane is airborne.

If assessors judge a threat credible, the crew is informed and the captain takes command of decisions for the aircraft, guided by company procedures. On the ground, that may mean an evacuation, while in the air the priority is to land as soon and as safely as possible.

As well as the fear and disruption to specific flights, each diversion can then affect the next flight scheduled to use that aircraft, the crew's working hours and passengers with connections.

The network effect can be significant. Philip Baum, a visiting professor of aviation security at Coventry University, told AP that a single diversion could cost an airline as much as $250,000, including replacement crews, missed connections and disruption to the rest of the day's schedule.

"Diversions necessitated by these bomb threats are a disturbance to a system designed for predictability and efficiency, and lead to increased workload for pilots, Air Traffic Controllers and airports," an EASA spokesperson said in emailed comments.

"As these staff have all have been trained to cope with such unexpected developments, the immediate safety threat from individual hoax threats is low. However, the higher number of diversions places an additional strain on the system."

National governments will already be looking at their aviation authority regulations and frameworks and there will be discussions at the European and global level, John Edwards, senior consultant at Gates Aviation in London, said by phone.

"Bomb threats to aviation have been going on for decades, what they'll need to be looking at is what's different. Why have there been so many, why have so many been considered sufficiently credible to warrant the kind of action that it's warranted?"

"There's a range of questions they should be able to work through fairly quickly to establish a broad-brush level of credibility. If there is some characteristic that is new, every time there is an event of this kind, it triggers a big collaboration to ensure recurrence is prevented going forward."

"In theory, he said, "the vast majority should be able to assessed and considered non-credible based on routine risk assessment processes and well established procedures."

However, Eric Schouten, a former intelligence liaison officer at the Dutch General Intelligence and Security Service and CEO of security consultancy Dyami, said that changes to existing procedural regulations would take some time and require balancing the views and responsibilities of countries, airports, safety authorities and the European Union, with geopolitics also playing a role.

"There is a high likelihood even more flights are going to be canceled or diverted. It's possible that next to be targeted could be railways and highways," he said.

"But they will figure it out. It's not the first time airlines have had to deal with hoax calls because there are always copycats."

"Whatever measures they come out with to address it, they won't share publicly" for risk of alerting the bad actors, he added.

Wilfried Covent said that while there would not be any quick changes to assessment protocol, "if this is really going on for ever and ever something will need to change or you'd have to stop flying completely."

"Based on what we've seen in the past with other kinds of threats, time goes on and the type of attack may change and move on," he continued, pointing to the wave of cyberattacks that has recently hit supermarket fridges and blood banks.
