#2975. America's newest small business owners with no employees are off to a hot start in AI era

It takes hundreds of billions of dollars for the tech giants to scale artificial intelligence, but it takes just a few AI tools for the smallest companies in the U.S. economy to get up and running with a cost-competitive business model.

Alex Willen expects the 12 e-commerce brands he operates with no employees to generate between $2.5 million and $3 million this year. Kristin Ginn left Microsoft in March and started trnsfrmAItn, an AI-adoption consultancy. Seven months later, she has roughly $250,000 in business received or contracted through year-end with no employees or regular contractors.

"The biggest misconception is that it's just about individual productivity; it's actually about leveraging AI to perform tasks that previously required a whole team," said Stanford economist Erik Brynjolfsson.

Early research on the impact of AI within the solopreneur business world bears watching.

According to Liya Palagashvili, a labor economist and director of the Labor Policy Project at George Mason University's Mercatus Center, new business formation is rising in direct correlation to applications unlikely to become employer firms.

In industries highly exposed to AI, business applications without early signs of hiring rose 26.8% from the first quarter of 2024 through the first quarter of 2026, from roughly 222,000 to 281,000 per quarter.

Over the same period, applications in those industries with stronger indications of eventually becoming employer businesses fell 6.4%. In construction and wholesale trade, which Palagashvili used as a comparison group, non-employer-type applications declined 0.4%.

"What AI is doing is giving those capabilities directly into the hands of the individual," she said.

Palagashvili cautioned that the findings are preliminary and descriptive and do not establish that AI caused the increase. The Census-based measure identifies applications without indicators associated with near-term payroll or hiring plans; it does not show that those businesses ultimately remained one-person firms or never hired employees.

But Willen shows what the economics can look like.

Anthropic's Claude helps with bookkeeping, customer service, advertising, and inventory across his businesses, while OpenAI image tools perform work that once went to photographers and designers.

Without AI, Willen said, "I'd probably need one other full-time person, plus I'd need to use contractors for photography, design, bookkeeping, and ad management."

A traditional product photography shoot could cost $2,000 to $5,000, and a designer $300 to $1,000 for new listing images, he said.

When AI directly eliminates one of those expenses, Willen said, "that money just goes into my pocket."

AI has also allowed him to pursue revenue channels he would not have previously considered. After acquiring Lone Trail, a hunting-gear brand selling binocular harnesses, packs, and other outdoor accessories, Willen said its Shopify site accounted for roughly 1% of sales. Rather than shut down the channel, he used ChatGPT and Claude to rebuild the site and AI to help run Meta advertising. In a recent 30-day period, he said, Shopify accounted for roughly 30% of Lone Trail's revenue.

"That's going to end up being at least six figures of annual revenue that would not exist without AI," Willen said.

Ginn's business shows how quickly a new entrant can reach meaningful revenue without first building an organization around it. She uses AI to help with proposals, pricing, strategy, and other work and is exploring an AI agent to perform part of a labor-intensive client process.

A survey conducted by accounting and invoicing software company FreshBooks identified similar behavior more broadly. Seventy-nine percent of solopreneurs said they had tried AI before hiring someone for a task they could not perform themselves (the same figure was 86% among when including microbusiness owners). AI allows them to "look much bigger than they are," FreshBooks CEO Shaheen Javadizadeh said.

But among existing small businesses, the available data tells another story. Gusto, a payroll and HR software company that serves small businesses, tracked businesses after they adopted AI and found their headcount was about 7% higher after a year than a comparable businesses that knew about AI but had not adopted it. Among firms with fewer than 10 employees, AI adopters grew headcount about 10% more than comparable non-adopters.

Willen's experience also highlights a tradeoff in the new business model: operating with fewer people does not eliminate reliance on larger technology platforms. Roughly 80% of its overall revenue still comes through Amazon, and he uses Meta and Shopify to expand direct sales. Ginn has no staff, but LinkedIn has been one of the biggest drivers of customers to her consultancy, she said.

Brynjolfsson said the proliferation of smaller producers can actually increase the value of the platforms that connect them with the buyers.

"There's this long tail of products, but at the same time, big platforms like Amazon are more powerful than ever because they aggregate all of these and they bring them together," Brynjolfsson said.

But the relationship is not as simple as labor savings flowing directly to Big Tech. Willen said some of the money he saves becomes profit, while Ginn spends less than $60 a month on paid AI tools.

AI can make it economical for more small producers to exist. And as core business production becomes cheaper, small businesses place greater focus on customer acquisition and distribution, Brynjolfsson said.

Brynjolfsson believes that while solopreneurs will thrive, use of AI will at the same time lead to the creation of even larger firms, on average, than exist today.

In a September research report titled "AI's Use of Knowledge in Society," Brynjolfsson and Zoë Hitzig, a researcher at the Anthropic Institute who studies the social and economic impacts of AI, argue that as AI codifies knowledge previously scattered among workers and enables more information to be aggregated and acted upon centrally, firms will grow larger on average, while industry concentration increases and local managerial autonomy declines. The researchers say early evidence is largely consistent with those patterns.

That's bad news for medium-sized companies. Brynjolfsson predicts AI could contribute to a "hollowing out" of midsize firms — very small teams capable of doing more at one end and giant organizations exploiting AI and scale at the other.

"The fat in the middle — the layers of management focused on internal coordination — will likely be significantly reduced," he said.

Adam Branch has experienced a version of that shift from inside two very different technology companies.

Before founding rhino.ai, an enterprise software company that uses AI to modernize legacy systems, Branch built Incentive Technology Group, a traditional technology services company that grew to roughly 500 employees. Under that model, winning more work meant hiring more people, he said.

"With rhino.ai, we're building AI software to do more of the work those teams used to do," said Branch.

Rhino.ai has roughly 50 employees. Branch estimates comparable modernization work can now be performed with three to four times the productivity per employee.

His team estimates labor represented roughly 85% of operating spending at ITG, and technology about 5%. At rhino.ai, Branch puts labor at roughly 40%, while technology, including cloud, software, and AI models, represents about 25%.

AWS sees a similar loosening of the relationship between revenue and headcount among AI-native startups. Its Engines of Growth research, an independent global study of 3,413 startup founders and senior leaders across 20 countries, found those companies grew revenue an average 156% annually versus 65% for startups overall, while more than half generated over $400,000 in revenue per employee. The report focused on companies under five years old that use AI in its most advanced forms.

"Growth used to be tied directly to how many people you could hire," said Ruba Borno, vice president of AWS Specialists and Partners. "Now founders can scale with technology, not just headcount."

Brynjolfsson does not see the result as simply Big Tech winning at everyone else's expense. Entrepreneurs can keep some of the productivity gains, consumers get more choice, and platforms benefit from supplying the technology and connecting more producers with customers, he said.

"The platforms and the people who are creating the tools are winning because they are able to sell them, the solopreneurs are winning because they are able to use them, and the consumers are winning because they are getting new products that they didn't get before," he said. "It's mostly a bigger pie, so it's a win-win-win — a triple win."

What AI may change is the shape of that bigger pie, he said.

"Whenever there's a technology change, a platform change, obviously it benefits the companies that are creating those platforms and technologies," Brynjolfsson said. "But it also benefits new entrants who can start fresh."

#2974. Santoli: AI bull market almost surely needs tech stocks to recover to keep advancing

The road to bull-market glory does not run through Walmart and Berkshire Hathaway and Eli Lilly.

There's been a virtual buying panic in traditionally defensive, high-quality stocks over the past few weeks, a near-equal-and-opposite offset to the selling pressure in semiconductors and AI-build-out spenders. The three stocks above — the only members of the $1 trillion market-cap club not in Big Tech – have collectively added $550 billion in market value in the past month, almost making up for what Alphabet has lost.

The Nasdaq-100 and the S&P 500 Low Volatility ETF (SPLV) are rushing toward one another in a satisfying picture of symmetry that nonetheless reflects an almost comical level of tactical rotation executed by an investment community accustomed to playing one factor against another.

While this to-and-fro has so far protected the S&P 500 as a whole from an appreciable setback for months, it has left it stymied around levels that date back to May — before we got two earnings-reporting seasons showing 30% annual profit growth.

A week ago here, I suggested the AI theme was nearing a make-or-break moment to stage a recovery or risk surrendering its leadership status and destabilizing the broader market. Thus far, this trade has failed to respond to moderate oversold conditions or a run of cosmetically strong hyperscaler quarterly results, further loosening its grip on the benefit of the doubt.

In stressed markets undergoing soul-searching about the long-term bullish thesis, prices often need to travel further to find buyers with conviction while the market action must disappoint dip-buyers to extinguish complacency. Hard to know if we're there, but presumably we're now closer.

The Nasdaq-100 closed Monday right at its 200-day moving average, off almost 11% from its record high. After an enervating failed Monday-morning bounce attempt, the Philadelphia Semiconductor Index finished down another 3.8% and settled under the late-July low that was set as the Situational Awareness hedge fund was finished liquidating.

"Markets did not get the memo that bearish seasonal pressure typically subsides in mid-October and instead ended the month with some of the most acute selling pressure that has been realized during the late-summer/early-fall period," notes JPMorgan technical strategist Jason Hunter. He's advising clients to remain on the defensive "until we see clear evidence of a bottoming process on the charts or until there are clear signs of capitulation."

This is a time-tested playbook, though many a sharp market pullback has ended without what would qualify as unqualified capitulation.

The market action is winning some plaudits for the way non-tech stocks have held up better, many groups benefiting from the "quality" rotation while abandoned consumer cyclicals get a reprieve from easing oil prices and the slide in the 10-year Treasury yield back below 5%.

This gives active managers plenty to do in playing against a highly concentrated and tech-dependent S&P 500. But I'll continue to argue that the bull market built on the AI story will almost surely need the AI story to get back on track to keep advancing. A prolonged period when the equal-weight S&P 500 outperforms the market-cap-weighted version is otherwise known – usually – as a bear market.

The AI-related stocks' weighting in the S&P 500 is off the highs but remains near 45%, according to Morgan Stanley.

As the chart shows, AI has wobbled relative to the rest of the market during this uptrend before reasserting itself.  But there's a trickier element to the current gut check: In prior phases of the buildout, the key limiting bottleneck gave investors something to buy in order to exploit it.

The initial scarcity of GPUs meant to own Nvidia above all else from 2023 through 2024. When cloud capacity, power and cooling were found to be the crucial scarce resources, hyperscaler stocks worked and the AI-adjacent industrials were drastically revalued. Then memory emerged as the chokepoint and Micron and Sandisk went stratospheric.

Now, as Nvidia's Jensen Huang has said, capital is the constraint and that not only leaves no asset to buy, but it raises the cost of capital broadly as the mega-caps go free-cash-flow-negative.

Overlapping feedback loops and scrambled correlations across the markets and the real economy have further sapped investor conviction. Treasury yields no longer move with oil but largely with AI-capex sentiment, with both in retreat lately. AI-stock setbacks also mute inflation momentum and the equity wealth effect, meaning fewer Federal Reserve rate hikes are anticipated now versus a month ago.

This all probably sounds like an elaborate trap, and it has become one for the previously crowded trades. Could this reflect the end game for the AI-exuberance market chapter, which as I keep pointing out peaked around the SpaceX IPO in June? Impossible to say for sure, but if it were, I'm not sure things would look much different to this point.

But I don't want to dismiss the possibility that a lot of the known challenges are largely reflected in prices.

I argued in August that we should assume the valuation peak for this cycle was in. We've now retrenched to a forward P/E of 18.5 on the S&P 500 from 23 a year ago and the Nasdaq-100 at 20x is at the slimmest premium to the S&P 500 valuation in nine years. I'm on board with the idea that the market is telling us companies are now "over-earning," but if nothing else there is less valuation risk now than in recent months.

Sentiment toward AI stocks is in the dumps. Funds' positioning in tech has retreated below neutral. Important trading lows this year have happened near the turn of the month on March 30 and July 29, and the Nasdaq-100's relative-strength reading is exactly as oversold as it was at those turns.

So, stay alert for possible relief bounces that could evolve into something more consequential if indeed the midterm elections pass in a way that allows traders to unclench a bit into the year's home stretch.

John Kolovos of Macro Risk Advisors on the latest reading: "Similar to valuation, sentiment is not a timing tool, but it is getting harder and harder to find any bulls, as a near-perfect storm of tight financial conditions is being met with tech earnings that have gone from bad to worse in the blink of an eye."

-Another good big-picture conversation on the practical business and investment implications of the next phase of AI proliferation on Patrick O'Shaughnessy's Invest Like the Best podcast, this one with investor Cliff Sosin. His comparison of AI infrastructure economics to internet connectivity providers and his argument for how most of the productivity boost will go toward "consumer surplus" rather than corporate profits are particularly interesting.

-For my birthday Monday, my colleague and friend Sara Eisen wondered how I would write a birthday message to myself. I immediately regretted suggesting she ask ChatGPT, which generated this credible and somewhat embarrassing result:

"Another year older, though the longer-term trend remains constructive. Some modest deterioration in the underlying internals, perhaps, but nothing that suggests a decisive breakdown.

"The candles on the cake are approaching historically elevated levels, though valuation concerns have persisted for years without derailing the advance. At this stage, the key question isn't whether I've gotten older — that's largely priced in — but whether the wisdom gains can continue to offset the inevitable compression in energy multiples.

"For now, I'm maintaining a cautiously optimistic outlook, with upside optionality, downside support from family and friends, and no immediate need for Fed intervention. Happy birthday to me. The secular bull market remains intact."

The stampede into perceived defensive stocks has been a crucial driver of Apple shares' massive outperformance over the Nasdaq-100 since the AI-capex agita flared up months ago. Apple heading into Monday's earnings report had outperformed the Nasdaq-100 by nearly 20 percentage points over the prior six months.

Its singular refusal to join the aggressive spending binge to build AI capacity preserved its free cash flow and made it the perfect counterweight to the capex trade, while inflating Apple's valuation to a 15-year high.

But its advantage in free-cash-flow yield has now narrowed, thanks to its stock-price gains and the collapse of peer shares.

#2971. Many workers lose unused FSA money — but could still come out ahead. Here's how

Health flexible spending accounts, or FSAs, are tax-advantaged accounts that let workers set aside pretax money to cover eligible health expenses. However, they're often use it or lose it, with unspent balances forfeited at the end of the plan year.

About 4 in 10 workers with health FSAs forfeited unused money in 2024, according to an October report from the Employee Benefit Research Institute, a nonprofit research organization. Among those FSA account holders, the average forfeiture was $404, down from $436 in 2023. The research examined more than 3.3 million accounts representing $3.9 billion in contributions.

Yet even workers who don't zero out their balance may still benefit from using an FSA, compared with what they would have spent using after-tax dollars to cover the same expenses.

"Even if someone forfeits FSA funds back to their employer, they can still come out ahead — relative to not participating in an FSA — if the amount they forfeited is less than the tax savings they realized," said Jake Spiegel, a senior research associate for EBRI and author of the study.

Nearly three-quarters — 72% — of state and local government workers and 47% of private-sector workers have access to a health FSA in 2026, according to the Bureau of Labor Statistics. That compares with 68% and 40%, respectively, in 2017.

FSAs differ from health savings accounts, or HSAs, which some people use in conjunction with high-deductible health plans. Although both let you set aside pretax money for health care expenses, HSAs let you roll over your balance from year to year, and even invest it.

Roughly a third of FSA users in the EBRI database face a strict use-it-or-lose-it provision, Spiegel said. The rest either are permitted by their employers to carry over an amount — up to $680 in 2026 — into the following year or are offered a grace period of up to 2.5 months after the deadline to incur additional expenses or face forfeiture. However, employers cannot offer both options.

While many plans have a Dec. 31 deadline for spending FSA money, some employers have deadlines falling in other parts of the year, said Rachel Rouleau, chief compliance officer for Health-E Commerce, parent company of online retailers FSA Store and HSA Store.

"Understanding these plan-specific deadlines is essential, so employees should check with their employer or FSA administrator to avoid forfeiting unused funds," Rouleau said.

Additionally, many employers give you extra time — typically three months after the deadline — to submit receipts for qualifying expenses that occurred during the previous plan year.

For 2026, the maximum contribution allowed to health FSAs is $3,400. In 2027, that amount will be $3,500, the IRS announced Thursday.

Part of the reason workers have forfeitures may be that they must decide how much they want to contribute to their FSA before the plan year begins.

"It's difficult to project how much you'll need to spend on health care, especially if you are particularly healthy and don't have a chronic condition with predictable prescription drug costs," Spiegel said.

Contributions to FSAs are withheld from paychecks and generally avoid federal taxes, as well as so-called payroll taxes — known as FICA — that go toward Social Security and Medicare. They also may avoid state and local taxes, depending on where you live.

To determine how much you're saving in taxes by using an FSA, you can add up those tax rates and apply the total to your contribution, said certified financial planner Sean Lovison, founder of Purpose Built Financial Services in Moorestown, New Jersey.

For example, say that your federal, FICA, state and local taxes total 35% and you contributed the maximum allowed — $3,400 for 2026. In this case, the calculation would result in $1,190 in estimated tax savings, which is "how much you're saving on taxes by contributing to your FSA," Lovison said.

The illustration assumes the worker receives no employer contribution to the FSA and the tax rate applies to the entire contribution.

"As long as the number you're forfeiting from your FSA is less than that number, you're going to come out ahead," Lovison said.

Of course, he said, "you don't want to walk away from FSA money, but at the same time, from a mathematical standpoint, you're still coming out ahead."

If you face a forfeiture, you can use it to help inform your decision on how much to put toward your FSA for 2027, if you haven't already made benefit elections for next year.

"Employees should look at their anticipated healthcare expenses over the coming year and consider both their daily health routines and expected annual expenses," Rouleau said. "This includes recurring prescriptions, doctor visits, copays, deductibles, dental and vision care, and other eligible out-of-pocket healthcare expenses for themselves and their eligible dependents."

Before you resign yourself to a forfeiture, keep in mind that there are also many ways to use the money on eligible expenses before the end of the year.

"There are thousands of ways consumers can responsibly spend down their FSA dollars, from everyday healthcare essentials to eligible telehealth services," Rouleau said.

Additionally, "many consumers are surprised to learn that a wide range of over-the-counter medications, menstrual care products, sunscreen, first-aid supplies and certain health-monitoring devices are FSA-eligible," Rouleau said.

That's in addition to doctor and dentist appointments, prescription drugs and other healthcare services such as acupuncture and addiction treatment, according to the IRS.

If you're unsure whether you have money remaining in your FSA or need to spend it by the end of the year, most plans offer an online portal where you can review your balance, submit claims and check deadlines. The FSA administrator's information is typically listed on the back of your FSA card, Rouleau said.

#2970. Knicks Bulletin: ‘I’m going to get some hate from the Swifties’

We were in the middle of a good, slow late-week break…

…only for Tyler Kolek, of all people, to force the Knicks PR into releasing a statement.

Here's that and more before the Knights host the Knickerbockers tonight.

On Karl-Anthony Towns' strong start and the areas where he still needs to improve:

"I say those two things because he's been doing so many things well for us. He's rebounding, he's running, he' s shooting the ball well from 3, he is passing when he doesn't go for the home run ball, he's communicating. The energy he's bringing is great."

On Towns' professionalism amid his contract situation:

"Yeah, I didn't have any doubt on it, you know? And obviously the contract stuff, that's something that Leon [Rose] and at the end of the day, Jim [Dolan], will handle. But for me as his coach, I can't even tell. I can't tell. He's played really well and not just during the games, but practices, too."

On Towns' pick-and-roll defense, turnovers and overall performance:

"Man, I tell you what, KAT's been great. He has been great in practice. Really everything. It would be easier for him to say the areas that he needs to be better in. And the first one is just putting his chest in front of the ball in pick-and-roll situations and activity in that regard. And then not looking for the home run pass all the time when he has the ball as our hub. And I say those two things because he's been doing so many things well for us.

"He's rebounding. He's running. He's shooting the ball well from three. He is passing when he doesn't go for a home run all the time. He's communicating. So there's the energy that he's bringing that's great . . . So I'm really pleased with the way that KAT is playing overall."

On unexpectedly giving Dalton Knecht playing time against Chicago:

"It's just a gut feel. Like, [Knecht's] on my, quote-unquote, minutes sheet. He's down for zero minutes, you know?"

On Dalton Knecht's impressive debut and encouraging him to play aggressively:

"He played great. I think the way he was rebounding and getting out in transition, and just telling him to be aggressive no matter what the situation is. And whether you know a player or not, being aggressive is important. And so aggressive mistakes are fine, so just stay aggressive, and he hit a big shot in the second quarter. He's going to be comfortable, and once he is, he's going to play even better."

On the Knicks' title defense and his motivation to win another one:

"I came into the season wanting to defend the championship at all costs. Like I said before, the city hasn't seen that trophy here in New York for 53 years, and all of us in this locker room, especially myself, don't intend for it to leave.

"Being the No. 1 pick, [reaching] the Finals and getting your title, and having to go out there and try to figure out how to do it again, that's enough motivation for me."

On his rebounding numbers and compensating for Mitchell Robinson's departure:

"I just always want to impact winning, and I always try to get every rebound possible for our team so we don't give up any second-chance points or we give ourselves more chances at second-chance points on offense.

"Obviously, with [Mitchell Robinson] gone, I had to pick up the effort, especially the rebounding effort. When you have one of the greatest offensive rebounders the NBA has ever seen in Mitchell Robinson, it's a team effort. I just want to do my part to help with rebounding."

On putting the Knicks ahead of his contract situation and personal interests:

"Me and Jalen [Brunson] are here to lead the team. I want to do my part as best as I possibly can. I can't allow outside distractions and ego to get in the way.

"We won last year because we put our egos to the side and we did what's best for this city, best for this team, this organization, and we sacrificed, and I'm always going to be willing to sacrifice for my brothers and this family that we've built here."

On focusing on winning rather than his contract and individual statistics:

"I want to do my job as the best possible way and I want to impact winning. And every single time I put that jersey on, I step on the court, I want to impact winning and help my team be the best they could be. So, you know, obviously I've got more work to do so I could help our team more. But I'm happy that right now recently, especially last game, we showed glimpses of that team from last year."

"At the Knicks games, I would say it's very performative. Because they just want to be seen at the game. They're not necessarily fans."

On the absolute fakest Knicks celebrity fan:

"Maybe Taylor Swift? I don't know if I've seen her at any other games. I guess she has a lot going on so she can't come all the time. But it's a little performative."

"I'm going to get some hate from the Swifties."

#2968. Mississippi Senate: Colom increases lead over Hyde-Smith to nearly 2,500 votes

Democratic challenger Scott Colom expanded his narrow lead over Sen. Cindy Hyde-Smith in the Mississippi Senate race on Friday afternoon.

As of 5:30 p.m. ET, Colom led Hyde-Smith by 2,498 votes, out of a total of 953,222 counted, with an estimated 97% of the votes in, according to the Associated Press.

That was 685 votes more than Colom's lead over Hyde-Smith when the day began.

Ty Pinkins, an independent candidate, had 20,028 votes, or 2.10% of the total.

The AP estimated that as of 4:45 p.m. ET, there were close to 30,000 votes that remained to be counted.

The Senate elections in Mississippi and Alaska are the only Senate races yet to be called by the AP. Democrats so far have flipped seven Senate seats from Republicans, meaning they will hold at least 54 seats out of the 100 in the Senate in January.

In Alaska, Democratic nominee Mary Peltola is leading incumbent Republican Sen. Dan Sullivan by 637 votes, a margin of just 0.29 percentage points. Peltola has slightly more than 48% of the votes cast, while Sullivan has slightly less than that.

Pinkins has told media outlets that he realized after a poll in April that he could not win the race, but that he decided to stay in to draw votes from Hyde-Smith.

Pinkins, who was the 2024 Democratic nominee in the Mississippi Senate race against Republican Sen. Roger Wicker, said a number of Republicans had contacted him to say they planned to vote for him because they did not want to vote for Hyde-Smith.

A final result may not be available until next week because more ballots can still arrive and be counted.

Mississippi's election procedures do not have an automatic recount provision.

Hyde-Smith's campaign set the stage for a potential legal battle over ballots on Thursday by demanding in a letter to county commissioners and circuit clerks in all 82 Mississippi counties that they immediately separate all provisional ballots cast in the election from regular and absentee ballots.

"These measures are necessary to ensure that each affidavit ballot's eligibility for counting and ultimate disposition can be verified," the letter said.

Lawyers for Colom's campaign responded with a letter of their own to the counties, calling the Republican's demands "unlawful and extraordinary measures."

"Those demands have no basis in Mississippi law, and if you depart from ordinary election procedures as you have been asked to do, you will violate Mississippi voters' rights and Mississippi election law," they said.

Provisional ballots, which are also known as affidavit ballots, are cast by a voter whose name does not appear on the poll book, who does not present an acceptable photo ID, or in several other situations.

The ballot is then placed in a sealed envelope and later examined by election officials to see if it can be cast, or the voter can return with a photo ID or evidence within five business days to show they are entitled to cast the ballot.

"Absolutely, I didn't think it would be this close," said Brian Anderson, a political science professor at the Mississippi University for Women in Columbus.

Anderson said that before the election, he thought Colom, a 43-year-old district attorney, might get into position to lose by just 3 or 4 percentage points, which would have been a very solid showing in a state that hasn't elected a Democrat to the Senate since 1982.

"I'm much more surprised" now that Colom has a chance to win, Anderson said.

If Colom defeats Hyde-Smith, 67, he would be the first Black senator from Mississippi since the post-Civil War Reconstruction Era.

"The Democrats have struggled here to create a pipeline of candidates," Anderson said. A Colom win "would galvanize the Democratic Party in a way it has not been," he said.

Neither candidate's campaign immediately responded to requests for comment from CNBC.

Hyde-Smith's X account, in a post Thursday afternoon, said, "Cindy Hyde-Smith is going to represent Mississippi in the United States Senate for the next six years."

"We believe thousands of votes remain to be counted from dozens of Republican counties, and we are confident those votes will reinforce her path to victory," the post said.

"Election officials are continuing the normal process of counting outstanding ballots, including absentee ballots. They must have the time to complete that work and ensure every lawful ballot is accurately counted and every Mississippian's voice is heard."

The post added, "We expect Democratic lawyers to pull out every stop, and we are prepared. Our focus is on completing the count and returning Cindy Hyde-Smith to the Senate to keep fighting for Mississippi values."

"Mississippi is the firewall, and this race is our line in the sand," the message said.

In a pair of ironic twists, if Colom wins, his victory could be attributed in some ways to actions taken by two Republicans: President Donald Trump and Hyde-Smith herself.

Supreme Court Justice Amy Coney Barrett, whom Trump appointed, wrote the court's 5-4 majority ruling in June that upheld a Mississippi law allowing mail-in absentee ballots postmarked by Election Day to be counted if they are received within five business days.

Those ballots could provide Colom a margin of victory. There are also an estimated 10,000 to 15,000 provisional ballots that require verification of eligibility to vote.

A lawyer for the Republican National Committee, whose lawsuit argued federal law barred counting votes received after Election Day, noted in a brief to the Supreme Court, "Democratic voters tend to mail their ballots later on average than Republican voters, which results in late-arriving ballots favoring Democratic candidates."

Trump at the time of the Supreme Court ruling called it "a tremendous loss" for voters' rights.

Hyde-Smith was appointed to her Senate seat in 2018 after the resignation of Thad Cochran and later that year won a special election for the seat. She was elected to a full term in 2020.

In 2023, she effectively blocked Colom's nomination by then-President Joe Biden to a post as a federal judge for the Northern District of Mississippi. Colom's nomination had the support of Mississippi's other Republican senator, Roger Wicker, and two former GOP governors.

Hyde-Smith said she opposed Colom's nomination for reasons that included his purported support for transgender students participating in girls' sports and because his campaign for district attorney had allegedly received money from billionaire George Soros.

Colom, in an April 10, 2023, letter to Hyde-Smith, said he was unaware before his hometown newspaper reported it that Soros had donated to a political action committee that had supported his campaign. He also wrote that he was not sure why Hyde-Smith had suggested he "opposed legislation to protect female athletes."

Colom concluded the letter by promising to be guided by the law, not his personal views on public policy, if he were confirmed as a judge.

"I hope you will reconsider your views about my nomination," he wrote.

Hyde-Smith did not reconsider her views and maintained her block on the nomination.

Colom announced his candidacy for the Democratic nomination for Senate in September 2025 and recently told CNN, "Me not having a federal judgeship is not a motivating factor for me at all" in running.

Anderson said Hyde-Smith ran "a low-visibility campaign," at least compared to Colom's, and that she focused on voters who traditionally would be expected to support her.

Hyde-Smith refused to debate Colom several times.

Colom "knows how to speak to people's interests, and Hyde-Smith was all about values, which is great for a lot of people, but it's all about the interests right now," Anderson said.

Those interests include concerns about affordability, the Iran war and corruption.

Colom is "bringing up all those," Anderson said.

Anderson noted that Colom's campaign had pounced on a remark Hyde-Smith made during a Feb. 22 interview with a local television outlet.

In the interview, Hyde-Smith answered a question about rising beef prices by saying, "You know, there so many proteins to choose from."

Colom's campaign featured a video clip of that remark in an ad featuring people reacting incredulously to Hyde-Smith's suggestion.

"Pick another protein?" one woman said in the ad. "She done lost her d--- mind. I'd rather pick another senator."

#2964. Patriots activate Mack Hollins from injured reserve on eve of Week 9

Mack Hollins will get the green light against the Green Bay Packers.

The New England Patriots officially activated the veteran wide receiver on the eve of Sunday's game at Gillette Stadium.

A calf injury had sent Hollins to injured reserve a week into October. After missing the NFL minimum of four games, he saw his 21-day practice window open as the calendar turned to November. Three limited sessions and a questionable game designation followed.

Hollins, 33, now returns as the eldest statesman on an active depth chart of four. With A.J. Brown ruled out for a second consecutive matchup due to a knee injury and Romeo Doubs still residing on injured reserve due to a shoulder injury, DeMario Douglas, Kyle Williams and Efton Chism III will make up the rest of the wideout room for the 4:25 p.m. ET kickoff.

At the time of his non-contact exit last month at Highmark Stadium, Hollins stood atop the offense with 20 targets, 15 catches and 209 yards through four appearances. The 6-foot-4, 221-pound North Carolina product finds himself 131 games, 56 starts, 223 catches, 2,828 yards and 17 touchdowns into a career that began with the Philadelphia Eagles in the fourth round of the 2017 NFL draft at No. 118 overall.

A Super Bowl LII champion, Hollins made subsequent stops with the Miami Dolphins, Las Vegas Raiders, Atlanta Falcons and Buffalo Bills before arriving in Foxborough on a two-year deal in 2025.

New England carried an open spot on the 53-man roster after releasing and retaining veteran cornerback Kindle Vildor on the practice squad ahead of the Green Bay visit. No standard elevations were made for Sunday afternoon.

Tight end Hunter Henry, offensive tackle Morgan Moses, cornerback Carlton Davis III and safeties Kevin Byard III and Brenden Schooler move forward with questionable game statuses, while guard Greg Van Roten will remain on injured reserve after also having his practice window open at the start of the week.

#2962. Winners & Sinners: Week 3

Welcome to Winners & Sinners, where we reflect on the week that was for the Milwaukee Bucks. Week 3 saw the Bucks go 0-3, with the team losing to the Minnesota Timberwolves, Boston Celtics, and Toronto Raptors, despite some valiant efforts that put them in positions to win. But who's deserving of the hardware?

Prior to the Raptors game, this was a legitimate conversation between Myles Turner and Tyler Herro. Then Turner pulled one of his all-too-common vanishing acts. Meanwhile, Herro put in his best playmaking performance as a Buck to make it a no contest. And although he's struggled in first quarters—zero first-quarter points this week—it hasn't really mattered. He heats up. Quickly. But as Nate O. said in our staff thread, "he's definitely more than just a microwave—he's an air fryer."

And so the nickname was born: Tyler Herro, the Air Fryer. Please make this a thing.

The Contenders: Myles Turner, Ryan Rollins.

The Decision: I was so ready to anoint Turner as the defensive champion following his showings against the Timberwolves and Celtics, where he was a force blocking shots, accumulating seven across the two games, and also doing yeoman's work on the boards (at least for him). Then he made it look like the Bucks would have to give up picks in any swap for Jakob Poeltl. Rollins had his moments too, especially when hounding Derrick White. But this belt ain't no participation award; folks need to take it. And so it's still collecting dust in the locker room.

This week's champion is Sausagemahoney, who raised a very thought-provoking question about the Bucks' decision to take Brayden Burries over Yaxel Lendeborg, who's since given the zombie Golden State Warriors new life—and who legend Chris Mullin thinks is "going to be a perennial All-Star and just an All-NBA type":

Truthfully, I'm just jealous I didn't pose the question for this week's Bucks Reacts Survey.

If, prior to the season, someone told you that Nate Ament would play fourth-quarter minutes for the Bucks—not the Herd—in the third week of the season, you'd have assumed it would come in garbage time. If they told you he would be a key contributor in a 22-point comeback, splashing an above-the-break three-pointer to cut the lead to two, you'd have called them crazy. Yeah, the Bucks didn't go on to win this one. And, yes, the Bucks are in the trenches. But moments like this give you a reason to believe.

No hesitation, no rim. No ceiling. Not for this rookie.

Whoever coined "Kuzmania" absolutely nailed it—even if it was originally intended as a positive. Like "mania's" psychiatric denotation, Kyle Kuzma's game is just all over the place far too often for a tenth-year veteran. This week's case in point came at the 2:59 mark of the fourth quarter against the Boston Celtics when, after coming back from a 22-point deficit and down only two, Kuzma decides to take a corner three with 19 seconds left on the shot-clock. 19! It wasn't even as if he was hot—he'd hit only two field goals all game. Of course, after it clanks off rim, the Celtics go on to collect multiple offensive rebounds on the next possession, before Derrick White makes an and-one layup to extend the lead to five. Game over. But the most infuriating thing isn't even that it cost the Bucks the win, it's that Kuzma is supposed to be a veteran presence teaching the Bucks' youth. Some lesson.

If Taylor Jenkins moving Brayden Burries into the starting lineup is a signpost of his willingness to tinker, then he has to do so elsewhere too. There is absolutely no reason to continue to start Kuzma and play him heavy minutes. None!

That's all for Week 3. Share your winners and sinners in the comments!

#2960. Tech companies face mounting child safety lawsuits while pushing AI into schools

Tech companies have spent years telling parents they're working to make their products safer for children. But ongoing jury trials, settlements and related litigation against the likes of Meta, Google and Snap suggest that they're not doing enough.

The latest examples landed this week. Snap confidentially settled a case claiming that its technology caused severe psychological distress to two teenage users. Meta, TikTok and YouTube previously settled with the plaintiffs in the same suit. And Google was sued this week by the parents of a child who allegedly suffered a psychological crisis after chatting with the Gemini AI chatbot in Google Classroom.

Concern is intensifying as artificial intelligence companies pursue trillion-dollar valuations, and established internet giants pour money into developing advanced AI models while pushing them into the classroom. Products and features intended to maximize engagement can have detrimental effects on users, thus warranting "broader systemic changes in tech design, particularly when children are using them," said developmental psychologist Kate Blocker.

"I think it's so so important that children's safety is being considered when these products — AI, social media, et cetera — are being designed, and not after the fact as these problems emerge," said Blocker, the director of research and programs for the nonprofit Children and Screens.

Snap's settlement this week, which spared CEO Evan Spiegel from testifying in a Los Angeles court, continues a general trend of tech companies attempting to shield themselves from public scrutiny due to the various high-profile child-safety related trials this year, said Joseph McNally, the director of emerging litigation at McNicholas & McNicholas.

The case was one of several lawsuits related to the social media personal injury trial that Meta and YouTube lost earlier this year. More trials are slated to commence in March.

"I view this settlement and the other settlements in this space as an effort by tech companies to turn a corner, not only because I think that there were real legal challenges, but also the reputation of their products, the reputation of their companies has really been hit," said McNally, a former acting U.S. attorney for the Central District of California.

"The parties are pleased to have been able to resolve this matter in an amicable manner," a Snap spokesperson said in a statement.

But there remains a federal case involving Meta, TikTok, Snap and YouTube and a litany of nationwide school districts, along with various state attorneys general suits. In August, Meta agreed to pay nearly $17 billion to settle with a group of state AGs in a child mental health case. Some AGs are still pursuing similar suits against Meta's rivals.

The social media lawsuits have helped inform parents and the public about how the industry designs products used by teenagers and children, McNally said.

"It's the technology arms race that continues," he said. "What is oftentimes missing is how you balance that participation in the arms race with utility and then product safety."

Google ran into more criticism this week for moving too fast. The New York Times reported on Tuesday that K-12 schools that don't allow use of Google's AI tools for students will lose access to search. Chris Phillips, vice president for education at Google, told the Times that, "Search and AI are deeply integrated."

That coincided with the new lawsuit against Google centering on a teen's alleged suicidal crisis that the parents attributed to the company's Gemini AI. The plaintiff's attorneys allege that "despite Gemini's known risks, Google hastily integrated" Gemini into Chromebook computers for school children.

"We are reviewing the claims in the lawsuit and we take this very seriously," a Google spokesperson said in a statement. "We've worked extensively with medical professionals and academic experts to build features that help young people find critical support and trusted resources when they need it."

Google and its internet peers have for decades been able to rely on Section 230.

Passed in 1996, the federal legislation has protected social media companies from being sued over content posted by their users. That's why plaintiffs' attorneys and state AGs have been focused on alleged harms stemming from the design of the tech platforms as opposed to their content.

Some plaintiffs are using the same tactics against AI companies. OpenAI faces several lawsuits from state AGs and plaintiff attorneys, taking cues from the social media suits in alleging that the design of ChatGPT can pose risks to children.

The litigation could become a growing financial risk as the company plans for a potential blockbuster IPO. OpenAI competitor Anthropic, which is planning to go public this month, highlighted the issue in its prospectus, even though the Claude creator prohibits children from using its product.

"This is a clear indication that tech companies anticipate more litigation risks than they did a decade ago," said Michele Neitz, a professor at the University of San Francisco School of Law.

Blocker said that the highly personalized nature of current AI-powered chat apps makes it "a little bit unpredictable" to determine exactly what the apps will say to kids. She stressed the importance of testing and evaluating these products for children's developmental safety from the start, "not after harms start happening."

This collective litigation has helped educate the public on the potential pitfalls of poor product design choices, Blocker said. And it could be a catalyzing force for change in the tech industry.

"Certainly there's a hope that this will motivate real systemic change, both from a regulation standpoint and internal company decision-making standpoint," Blocker said.

If you are having suicidal thoughts or are in distress, contact the Suicide & Crisis Lifeline at 988 for support and assistance from a trained counselor.

#2957. What midterm election blue wave rebuke of Trump means for renewable energy

As recently as 2022, many leaders in corporate America and the investment community were singing the praises of ESG — environmental, social, governance — as the foundation of stakeholder capitalism. It didn't take long for that tune to change, for understandable reasons.

President Trump didn't just make "ESG", "sustainability" and "climate change" dirty words in his "green new scam" rhetoric. The Trump administration's rollback of environmental regulations, elimination of clean energy subsidies and tax credits, defunding and halting of wind and solar projects, and embrace of the fossil fuels industry accelerated the backlash.

But renewable energy investing remains alive and well, notably among public and private funders of infrastructure projects and clean technology innovation. And following the midterm elections that gave climate-friendly Democrats control of the House and Senate, the prospects of reviving the clean energy agenda suddenly brightened.

Clean energy and solar stocks rallied the day after the election results, which "potentially reduces the risk of further political interference with renewable energy incentives and provides greater protection for the long-term economics of U.S. wind, solar and battery storage investments," Barclays analysts led by Peter Crampton wrote in a note.

While Trump can veto legislation to restore subsidies and credits and revive projects, and continue to issue executive orders on energy policy, some Republicans may be more inclined to help Democrats override the president in order to appeal to voters outraged over high energy prices — a sentiment expressed in the passage of multiple state initiatives to impede the build-out of electricity-hungry data centers.

The blue wave also shines a spotlight on a bipartisan permitting-reform bill awaiting Senate approval. Several progressive Democrats want to put off the vote until the new Congress convenes in January, giving them time to rewrite provisions favoring renewables projects over fossil fuel ventures.

Ultimately, the midterms could improve the outlook for investors focused on clean energy. That is likely to start with impact investors who specifically seek to generate a competitive financial rate of return alongside positive, proactive social and environmental impact. Opportunities can be found through public equity investments like mutual funds and ETFs that are constructed to invest in clean energy companies. But many investors also seek out pure-play clean energy positions offered through fixed income, private equity, private credit, venture capital, real assets and community-focused investments.

"There are many different approaches that could lead impact investors to be thinking about clean energy investments," said Amit Bouri, who's retiring as CEO of the Global Impact Investing Network at year's end. GIIN's 2026 survey of impact investors found that 56% have funds invested in clean energy and 25% plan to increase those investments over the next five years. The survey was conducted prior to the midterms.

"Clean energy infrastructure can be really compelling from an investment standpoint, because the financial thesis and impact thesis are mutually reinforcing," said Justina Lai, chief impact officer at LNW, a wealth management advisory firm. LNW has about $3.5 billion of its total $12.1 billion assets under management invested in impact strategies. She added that it's challenging to break down the number of clients who are specifically focused on clean energy and clean tech. "Climate change or climate action writ large is likely the most common impact priority that we encounter with our clients," she said.

While it might be hard to focus on the potential in the renewable energy sector in 2026 with energy commodities leading all sectors of the market as crude oil prices surged, some of the factors Lai cites should be on the radar of investors beyond the impact investing space.

She pointed to the nation's growing electricity demand — partly tied to data centers — its aging and constrained grid infrastructure, the increasing need for energy storage, the effects of geopolitics on energy security and rising energy costs as major factors driving clean energy investing. Simultaneously, though, because of declining costs and continuing maturity of many clean technologies, "we know that renewables are the lowest cost form of new-build generation, even on a fully unsubsidized basis," she said.

The proof is in the gigawatts. The U.S. is on pace to add a record 89 GW of clean energy capacity by the end of 2026, with installations projected to exceed that level in 2027, according to BloombergNEF's latest US Clean Energy Market Outlook. In just the first half of this year, the Environmental Defense Fund and Atlas Public Policy recently reported, 11.5 GW of solar, 8.3 GW of battery storage and 6.3 GW of wind power were installed.

U.S. investment in clean energy and transportation alone totaled $75 billion in the second quarter of 2026, the second-highest quarterly figure on record and up 22% from the previous quarter, according to the Clean Investment Monitor, a joint project of Rhodium Group and MIT's Center for Energy and Environmental Policy Research.

The supply chain has amped up, too. By the third quarter of this year, manufacturing capacity for solar modules, wind blades and nacelles had expanded by roughly 50% from the first quarter of 2025, reflecting new facilities that have come online, the CIM reported in October. And over the past four quarters, $277 billion was invested across the country in the manufacture and deployment of renewable energy, non-fossil fuel vehicles, building electrification and carbon-management technology.

"Investment has consistently been very strong in solar and storage at the utility scale," said Hannah Hess, director, energy and climate at Rhodium. That makes sense from a bottom-line perspective, she said, "particularly when some of the deadlines for these projects have changed for them to be eligible for tax credits."

Hess is referring to provisions in the One Big Beautiful Bill Act, which significantly hastened the sunsets and deadlines for federal clean energy tax credits compared to the original timeline set by the Biden administration's Inflation Reduction Act. Solar and wind projects had to begin construction by July 4 of this year or be placed into service by December 31, 2027, to claim standard investment or production tax credits.

Meanwhile, even with the Trump administration's concerted efforts to rescind much of the $1.6 trillion set aside for clean energy under Biden's Bipartisan Infrastructure Law and the IRA, nearly $600 billion remains available, according to a Politico analysis. Keeping that third of the funding pipeline open bodes well for clean energy developers and investors, especially considering the midterm results.

"Despite the gutting of the IRA, our long-term thesis and our clients' long-term views on clean energy remain the same," said Roraj Pradhananga, chief investment officer at Veris Wealth Partners, an advisory firm that specializes in impact investing. "If we look at the volatility and risk-adjusted returns of the traditional energy sector, in the long run, shifting to renewable energy makes the most [economic] sense," even when taking the tax credits out of the equation, he said.

Veris manages $3 billion across asset classes, Pradhananga said, using various vehicles, including public markets, private debt and infrastructure funds. "Climate solutions tend to be the largest allocation for our clients. We recently allocated to strategies from GPs like Closed Loop Partners, Streetlife Ventures, SJF Ventures, New Energy Capital and Sunwealth."

The proliferation of AI data centers was a hot-button issue in the midterms. Voters in 10 states weighed in on 41 local initiatives, which included complete bans on new construction, pausing ongoing and planned facilities and restrictions on energy consumption. All but one passed. And although newly elected governors in three states — Ohio, Texas and Georgia — ran on platforms critical of data centers, they're not calling for permanent bans.

For instance, Gina Hinojosa, governor-elect of Texas, said during a CNN interview on November 5, "We will have laws to protect Texans, and we need to stop what we are doing with data centers until we have those protections." She also emphasized giving local communities a meaningful say in whether and how facilities operate. That echoes Pennsylvania Governor Josh Shapiro's cautious approach to data center development in that state. In August, he issued an executive order that places a host of restrictions on developers.

The upshot for AI companies may be a slowdown in new data center construction, increased input from local residents and officials, and self-financing for electricity and water usage. Yet they are not expected to change their long-term expansion plans. A few days before the election, Goldman Sachs said, "While U.S. data center development faces increasing political and community opposition, project data and our updated capacity estimates suggest that the...growth outlook through 2027 remains largely unchanged." The Wall Street firm forecast data center power demand to grow 38% or 12 GW in 2026 and 38% or 17 GW in 2027.

On Friday, OpenAI's CFO told CNBC the company will not be changing its investment plans despite the election results. White House economic advisor Kevin Hassett expressed a similarly optimistic view of continued investing on CNBC on Friday. "We respect the localities that decide that's what they want to do," Hassett said. "But America is a big place, and right now, the data centers scheduled over the next couple of years aren't affected by these ballot initiatives."

From another perspective, the risks associated with AI and data centers could be beneficial to renewable energy investments. "If anything, sustainability concerns around AI actually reinforce their interest in climate action," Lai said. "The growth in AI and data centers could drive significant new fossil fuel generation, so our clients are motivated to ensure that more of that [electricity] demand is met by renewables."

That's true for many of Veris' clients, too, Pradhananga said. But he also acknowledged that because natural gas is a prominent fuel source for large-scale utilities, as well as behind-the-meter LNG turbines that power many data centers, "there are some [of our] clients who view this as part of the transition" from fossil fuels to renewables, espousing the often-mentioned "all of the above" approach to the changeover. "This is where we do have exposure to utilities in [our] public markets portfolio."

Public consternation over data centers, a Democratic-controlled Congress come January and, at least for now, a voting map across the country that has swung significantly towards the Democratic Party ahead of the 2028 presidential election, could create a trifecta of tailwinds for investors in renewable energy and clean-tech. And while any moratorium on data centers could impact renewable project development on a case-by-case basis, it might also promote investments in transmission grid upgrades that allow utility-scale solar and wind installations to tie into existing and proposed data centers.

It could also speed up research and development of emissions-free power generation, energy-efficiency and other technologies that climate-tech VCs are funding. Due to the AI boom, renewable energy companies have faced stiffer competition in recent years for venture capital dollars previously targeting climate change and decarbonization. But climate-tech VC hit $26 billion in the first half of 2026, a 55% increase year over year, according to market-intelligence firm Currence.

"There are very interesting infrastructure opportunities in [climate investing], including advanced fusion ventures, floating offshore wind turbines and solar plus storage operations," said Adam Wolfensohn, a clean-energy investment advocate and advisor. "All of these technologies keep getting better and cheaper, unlocking so much more potential for renewables and new financial structures. It's a rich time to be looking at this space."

Among the guiding resources the GIIN offers to its 350 member organizations in 40-plus countries, including 126 in the U.S., is a recently launched climate solutions investing framework. "It's designed to help investors make sense of the landscape of climate solutions and to ensure they have rigor around the actual climate impacts underpinning an investment strategy," Bouri said. "A lot of things have green labels on them, but what we are really focused on is what drives real results for people and the planet."

#2955. Fannie Mae, Freddie Mac rally as Ackman presses for conservatorship exit

The midterm elections could be a turning point for the release of Fannie Mae and Freddie Mac from government control, according to billionaire investor Bill Ackman.

Investors may agree with him, if the rally in both stocks is any sign.

The Federal National Mortgage Association, known as Fannie Mae, and Federal Home Loan Mortgage Corporation, or Freddie Mac, have each jumped more than 60% from Oct. 14 to Monday's close. The stocks were both up roughly 3% on Tuesday.

Ackman, the founder of Pershing Square Capital Management, has long pushed for the two entities to be released from federal conservatorship.

"The reason why it's not being touched right now is, you know, midterms," he said in an interview with CNBC's "Squawk Box."

Ackman, who owns both names through Pershing Square, believes Freddie and Fannie have the potential to increase "10-fold from current share prices," he said in his quarterly letter to shareholders, issued on Monday.

Fannie and Freddie buy mortgages from banks and other lenders, and then package them into mortgage-backed securities, known as MBS. Their operations are critical to the housing market, providing liquidity to banks and creating a secondary market for MBS.

The entities were placed into conservatorship in September 2008, during the financial crisis. The stocks were delisted from the New York Stock Exchange in 2010 and have traded over the counter since then.

Ackman wants the conservatorship to end, arguing the entities would be worth multiples of their current valuation if they were freed from government control.

President Donald Trump has supported ending the conservatorship of Fannie and Freddie. But it remains politically and economically contentious.

The entities operate under strict capital threshold requirements and would need to be recapitalized, which could happen through issuing common equity. However, the Treasury Department would need to figure out an exit plan for its roughly $388 billion ownership stake in the entities through preferred securities and warrants.

Congress may also need to play a role, passing legislation to restructure the government's role in mortgage financing and home-loan guarantees.

Nonetheless, Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent recently said it could happen before Trump's term ends, telling 2Way's "The Morning Meeting" podcast that the administration will tackle the ownership structure "next year, the year after." He said affordability will be guiding the decision.

One point of controversy is whether ending the conservatorship of Fannie and Freddie would increase the spreads on MBS over Treasuries, effectively making mortgages costlier. Bessent has sought to reassure the market that wouldn't happen.

"Whatever we do is going to be guided by making sure that the spread on [agency] mortgages … over the Treasury does not increase," Bessent said.

Ackman argues that privatizing the GSEs would help insulate them from political meddling. "Fannie and Freddie are these massive, enormously profitable institutions where whichever political party controls them…if they're still in conservatorship, can influence the outcome of elections," he told CNBC.

In one scenario, he explained, Democrats win the White House, conservatorship is still in place, and Democrats push Fannie and Freddie to lower credit standards in order to expand home ownership and affordability.

"If they're going to move more aggressively into borrowers with lower credit quality, for example, to achieve a political objective, that's going to have an impact on trillions of dollars outstanding mortgage securities," Ackman said. "It's going to have an impact on overall credit spreads."

MBS investors want certainty about how the entities will be run, he noted.

"That only gets resolved in a world in which they exit conservatorship, they become publicly traded companies that are owned by institutional and retail investors," Ackman said. "We set the rules. In conservatorship, the president can do anything."

#2953. Patriots up-to-date depth chart: Who is on the roster right now?

The New England Patriots' regular season is underway, which also means that their roster and practice squad will undergo near-constant change. For reference, between the opener and regular season finale alone last season, the eventual conference champions made 102 different transactions.

It remains to be seen how many there will be in 2026, but things can get confusing quick. So, in order not to get lost amid any potential movement, here is our handy up-to-date Patriots roster and depth chart.

Hey, a win! The Brooklyn Nets entered Wednesday night's game against the Charlotte Hornets searching for their first home win of the season. Thankfully for them, it finally came thanks to a big fourth quarter run as Brooklyn came away with a 114-104 victory.

Now, Brooklyn takes that momentum on the road, beginning a four-game trip against an Atlanta team coming off an offensive explosion. The Atlanta Hawks are hoping to go far in the postseason this year and position themselves for a promising future. They helped the cause with a 139-132 victory over the Washington Wizards on Friday night.

Matchup: Brooklyn Nets (2-6) vs. Atlanta Hawks (6-4)

Watch: YES Network, DAZN, NBA League Pass

Questionable – Drake Powell (G-League assignment)

OUT – Mouhamed Gueye (left foot fracture)

OUT – Keshon Gilbert (left knee inflammation)

OUT – Asa Newell (left great toe hyperextension)

This night one of a back-to-back for Brooklyn. We'll see if anything changes before tip-off.

The Nets are on the first night of a back-to-back and first game of a four game road trip. They'll head to Dallas for a Sunday evening matchup with the Mavericks.

The Hawks are on the second night of a home back-to-back. They wrap up their brief three game homestand on Monday night against the Los Angeles Lakers. They hope to do something big this spring, but they have their doubters. From Ricky O'Donnell at the mothership:

"This team doesn't have enough force going to the basket, and the defense isn't anything special despite generating a lot of takeaways and forcing misses from three. The Hawks look mid as always."

It takes time to get used to someone in a new role. We've spent so much time with Nickeil Alexander-Walker being a solid bench role player that it's still somewhat surprising to see him operate as a high(er) usage wing as a starter. Alexander-Walker won the Most Improved Player award last year and he's carried that success over to this year. He's averaging a career best 22.3 points per game, and most impressively is getting to the line over four times a game. When Johnson returns, we'll see how the Hawks look to climb the standings.

Nolan Traore keeps stacking good days together. The second year guard had another good game as a starter on Wednesday night and he's making a great case to remain the starter once Egor returns. Traore can get to the rim and is second on the team in drives per game.

We'll get to watch a rookie who finally made his way into the starting lineup.

CJ McCollum requested that he come off the bench and the kid gets to start. With the Hawks looking to establish their future, a selfless decision like that from McCollum will go a long way in establishing Flemings as a key player going forward. For Atlanta, finding out what they have in Flemings will go a long way in determining their future. He's played over 23 minutes in four out of the last five games and with this being night two of the b2b, we'll see how long Quin Snyder plays him for.

Mikel Brown Jr is not on the injury report, so he'll play tonight and will more than likely sit tomorrow night in Dallas. The Nets are continuing to build Brown Jr and trust that his growth will lead to an exciting future. The drives are there, but if the three pointers start to become consistent, the sky is the limit for him and the Nets. It's all about making the most of the opportunities that are available to you.

Last weekend, the Atlanta Dream fell short in Game 7 of the WNBA Finals against the Golden State Valkyries. The last do-or-die game to decide the W Finals was a pretty celebratory occasion around these parts.

#2949. Will this week's blue wave election usher in a Democratic president in 2028?

In surfer slang, a wave that lasts a long time is a "runner." A key question for investors is whether the blue wave that washed over the country Tuesday was that kind of wave, and could it ferry Democrats to victory in 2028.

Statistically, the answer is it should have little effect.

CNBC analyzed elections since 1938 and looked at the biggest midterm wave elections where the party in the White House lost seats to determine whether they portend anything about the presidential election two years later.

Tuesday's election was the biggest House seat loss for the incumbent party in the White House since 2018, but it only barely makes the historical Top 10. It ranks 10th in CNBC's analysis using an estimate of 41 House seats gained by the opposition party as close races are still being tallied. The largest loss came in 1938 when Democrats lost 72 House seats amid a renewed recession, opposition to court-packing by President Franklin Delano Roosevelt and the president's battle with conservatives in his own party. Roosevelt went on to win reelection two years later in 1940.

In more modern times, Republicans swept to power in the House in 2010 two years into Barack Obama's first presidential term, with Democrats losing 64 seats, though retaining control of the Senate. Obama went on to reelection two years later. Of the top four wave elections, which all occurred before a president's final term, the incumbent went on to win reelection each time.

But for the next five waves, the incumbent party lost, including John F. Kennedy beating Richard Nixon in 1960 after Republicans lost seats under President Dwight Eisenhower in 1958; and Joe Biden beating Donald Trump in 2020 after Republicans lost seats in 2018.

Jay Campbell, partner at Hart Associates, the Democratic pollster for the CNBC All-America Economic Survey, said the reason midterm waves may not carry forward is the importance of individual characters in presidential elections.

"A lot can happen in two years. And while presidential elections are certainly subject to the overall political environment, they are also very much about each party's nominee," Campbell said. "If candidate quality matters in congressional elections, it matters 1,000 times more in presidential ones."

It's a long way to 2028 and this wave, gnarly as it may have been, could statistically peter out just as easily as it could wash Democrats back into the White House.
