#2912. Oso Ighodaro is starting; is that a good thing?

The Phoenix Suns moved Oso Ighodaro into the starting lineup for Thursday's win against the Orlando Magic. Many called for a change to the lineup (or multiple changes, which is another article for another day). He is a somewhat polarizing player, with staunch fans and outspoken skeptics. I'll make a case for this being absolutely the right move for this team, right now.

Khaman Maluach is learning game by game. Working on his body (and hopefully hands) to get stronger. He has an awesome frame and, at 20 years old, is just beginning to learn the nuances of NBA success. He has a mostly high effort level and wants to be good. However, the NBA is littered with players thrust into action before developing properly – and then losing confidence, picking up bad habits. Time on the court in meaningful minutes is needed to progress toward the objective of becoming a solid NBA starting center. The current situation with Mark Williams out well into 2027 allows for consistent 15-20 minute nights. Handing the "starter" keys could put him on a path more closely aligned to former Suns top-10 pick Josh Jackson (sorry to those with draft PTSD here).

Back to Oso. The Phoenix starting lineup needs more connectivity and flow on offense? Check. Struggling horrifically on defense? Check. Per HoopsJunkie.io, Ighodaro's on/off defensive rating of -17.2 points per 100 possessions ranks EIGHTH in the NBA! This is in spite of being a below-average defensive rebounder (141st in the NBA in DREB%). Keep in mind, he suffered a right oblique strain in the opener and is likely just rounding back into form now. I've referenced DARKO several times as a great tool to project overall player value at a moment in time.

Through last season, his projected value was steadily growing. Once fully healthy, it is reasonable to expect that progression to continue. He looks bigger and stronger. His turnovers are down. While the results are not there yet at the free-throw line, he looks smoother (and commentators have noticed the same). With a DARKO of 1.0, compared to a -1.6 for Maluach, the model predicts more positive impact on winning in the present.

Oso and Ryan Dunn, to an even greater extent early this season, are developing players. They show what development can look like for players drafted 40th and 28th, respectively. With his raw talents (drafted 10th) and willingness to work, Maluach's ultimate success depends on development done the right way. This franchise needs Man Man to get there for the long run – and needs Oso right now to stabilize a starting lineup that was in disarray.

#2910. Risk of Iran war escalation remains high despite Democratic sweep in U.S. midterm elections

The U.S. and Iran are careening toward renewed war despite growing hopes this week that the Democrats' decisive midterm election victory will bring the conflict to an end, analysts say.

Crude oil prices fell more than 16% over the last five trading days after U.S. Vice President JD Vance publicly blamed the Republicans' stinging loss on the war. Vance's remarks raised questions about whether the administration still has support to continue the conflict with Iran.

The Democrats plan to use their comfortable majorities in both chambers of Congress to pass a war powers resolution that demands President Donald Trump end the war. But tensions in the Middle East have not eased since the election, and the Democrats aren't sworn in until January, the analysts say.

"Throughout the week, we were struck by the divergence between deepening escalation concerns in the Gulf and the headline-driven off-ramp optimism of market participants thousands of miles away," Helima Croft, head of global commodity strategy at RBC Capital Markets, told investors in a note Friday.

Late Saturday, U.S. Central Command said it disabled a St. Kitts-flagged ship after the tanker ignored multiple warnings and attempted to leave the region after visiting an Iranian port, according to media reports. Iran state media also reported on explosions on Qeshm Island in the Strait of Hormuz, as well as several blasts occurring in the port of Jask. CNBC couldn't independently confirm the reports.

Several oil tankers have also come under attack near Oman since the election, killing one seafarer, according to maritime security agencies. Iran's Houthi allies in Yemen continue to fire missiles at neighboring Saudi Arabia.

Meanwhile, the U.S. military buildup continues, with a third carrier strike group en route to the region. The consulting group Rapidan Energy sees an 80% chance that fighting will resume as diplomacy stalls.

Trump told reporters Thursday that he will make a decision soon on whether to attack Iran again.

"I would rather not destroy the rest of the country if possible but maybe I'll have to," Trump said in the Oval Office. "You'll know soon enough."

"We're locked and loaded," the president said. "If we have to do the rest of the Iran thing, we're very much locked in."

Crude oil prices will vault above $100 a barrel again in response to renewed fighting, said Bob McNally, president of Rapidan and a former energy advisor to President George W. Bush. Brent crude, the international benchmark, traded above $97 on Friday, while U.S. crude was around $90.

"President Donald Trump is determined as a legacy item, almost at all costs, to prevent Iran from getting a nuclear weapon, having them give up their nuclear capabilities," McNally told CNBC's "Squawk Box Asia" on Friday.

"The Iranian regime, on the other hand, is not willing to do it," the analyst said. The escalation dynamic remains in place so long as that core difference between the U.S. and Iran persists, he said.

The risk of war is still alive, especially since Trump no longer faces future elections, said Amos Hochstein, a former energy advisor to President Joe Biden. Gasoline, diesel and jet fuel prices would all surge as a consequence, Hochstein told CNBC's "Power Lunch" Friday.

Tehran has been taunting the Trump administration since the election. The social media account of Iran's Supreme Leader Mojtaba Khamenei said the "process of the U.S.'s collapse has accelerated" after the midterm results.

And Congress is unlikely to pass a war powers resolution until Democrats take control in January. Trump can veto such a resolution anyway.

The main leverage Congress will have is the power of the purse, Croft said. Government funding expires on Dec. 11, and negotiations for another continuing resolution could drag into 2027, she said. This would give Democrats more leverage when they take power in January, the analyst said.

"Importantly, deferring funding decisions would not immediately constrain Trump's ability to conduct military operations, but could significantly reshape the administration's longer-term funding outlook," Croft told investors.

Trump discussed diplomatic efforts to de-escalate tensions in the region with the emir of Qatar during a Friday phone call, according to Qatar's state news agency.

An unnamed senior Iranian official told Reuters on Thursday that Tehran sent a nuclear proposal to the U.S. through Qatar. Tehran will only discuss the proposal after the U.S. meets its demands, the Iranian official told Reuters.

Trump seemed to dismiss the diplomatic efforts Thursday.

"Iran will never have a nuclear weapon," the president said. "They're not doing anything without our approval. It doesn't make any difference what they're looking at," he said of the diplomatic efforts.

#2909. CNBC's experts on whether the AI trade is ending and what investors should do next

Amid a powerful rout in tech stocks, investors told CNBC on Thursday that something may be fundamentally breaking in the AI trade following the big success of Democrats in Tuesday's midterm elections and the threat they try to slow the data center buildout.

Democrats are more aggressively anti-data center than Republicans, and Wall Street is quickly redoing its math what any restrictions they implement could mean for the related companies' future growth.

"Right now, investors are questioning that $1.2 trillion of capex that we forecast for next year," Peter Boockvar, chief investment officer at One Point BFG Wealth Partners, told CNBC. "Well, maybe that's not going to happen."

Boockvar believes this could be the start of the end of the AI trade as we know it.

Whether it's completely over or not, investors need to be reassessing how they're planning to play the AI trade going forward, the analysts said.

The Nasdaq Composite dropped 5% on Thursday and fell into correction territory, off by more than 11% from its record high set last month. The iShares Semiconductor ETF dropped 11% on Thursday, its biggest decline since during the Covid breakout in March 2020. The Global X Artificial Intelligence & Technology ETF (AIQ) is down 6% on the day and down 15% over the past month.

"There has been a huge change in the outlook for AI infrastructure buildout," Eric Johnston, head of equities at Cantor Fitzgerald, told CNBC. "The entire economy and the consumer and earnings growth are all levered to that buildout."

The selling Thursday spilled over to non-AI stocks with the Dow Jones Industrial average down more than 1,600 points.

Josh Brown pointed to the 4.8% yield for the 10-year Treasury yield, saying a compelling risk-free rate is causing investors to reconsider taking the risk in these volatile AI stocks.

"You don't need to be at 80% U.S. stocks and have 60% of that exposure be tech," Brown said.

Many investors believe the AI trade isn't over completely, investors just need to get more selective.

"The AI buildout just got harder," said Rob Sechan, Newedge Capital Group Founder & Managing Partner:

"Nobody voted to make compute less scarce," Sechan said, citing Microsoft's Azure cloud business growing 48% year over year in its most recent third quarter. That level of growth suggests its capacity constrained, not demand constrained, he said.

"When new capacity gets harder to build, existing capacity becomes more valuable," Sechan said.

Richard Clode, portfolio manager at Janus Henderson, noted a few bright spots in tech on Thursday despite the highly correlated downward movements in the big tech indices.

"Motorola Solutions had some good results," he said. "We're seeing a little bit of rotation into some of the … internet names...Sony reported some good results, and again they were up strongly overnight."

"So there are some brief pockets of respite from this, but obviously pretty painful across the board today," he said.

Some experts believe the selling was overdone and investors could start nibbling.

This "indiscriminate selling" is a "buying opportunity," said Larry Adam, Raymond James chief investment officer. If investors "take a longer term perspective, which is what we do, I think the valuations at today's levels will tend to prove to be a good buying opportunity," he said, urging investors to look at the fundamentals.

Victoria Greene, chief investment officer of G Squared Private Wealth, said she saw some strong supports around the S&P 500's 200-day moving average and was on the lookout for a market bounce following Thursday's rout.

"Typically, the average return is plus about 180 basis points the next day," she said. "The question we have to ask ourselves: Is this a pullback in an uptrend, which is what I believe? Or is this the end-all, be-all [and] the wheels are coming off and everybody just needs to buy T-bills? I don't think we're there yet."

#2908. No. 24 Florida stifled by Oklahoma as Florida loses for fourth time in five games

GAINESVILLE, Fla. — Florida's October freefall continued into November, a month coach Jon Sumrall hoped would generate positive momentum for the future.

A 23-7 loss to Oklahoma on Saturday night in the Swamp was the latest misstep for the No. 24 Gators, now losers of four of their past five games after cruising to a 4-0 start in September.

A 6-yard touchdown pass from John Mateer to Isaiah Sategna III with 4:56 remaining in the game capped a 20-play, 89-yard drive spanning nearly 13 minutes. Oklahoma's longest scoring drive in 20 seasons ended Florida's chance to pull out a win.

Most of the night, the Sooners' first visit to the Swamp in the program's storied history lived up to its billing as a defensive battle between two desperate teams. The game featured only two plays longer than 20 yards.

The Gators offense rarely found its rhythm during sophomore Tramell Jones Jr.'s first home start, and just the second start of the 19-year-old's Florida career.

The Sooners overran Florida up front as the Gators struggled to sustain drives or generate a consistent running game. Facing a Sooners' defense with All-SEC talent at all three levels, Florida (5-4, 3-4 SEC) finished seven of its 10 drives with less than 25 yards of production.

With a reported sellout crowd of 90,442 on hand for their first SEC night game of the season, the Gators ended the evening with just 203 yards, 11 first downs and ran just 47 plays. They scored a single touchdown for the second straight game. The Sooners (5-4, 3-3) sacked Jones three times and intercepted him twice.

Scoring opportunities were scarce for Florida, while mistakes and miscalculations were costly.

After Oklahoma took a 3-0 lead on a 41-yard field goal by Tate Sandell on its opening drive, the Gators reached Oklahoma's 42. But right tackle Caden Jones, starting in placed of injured Emeka Ugori, committed consecutive holding calls to negate a pair of 18-yard gains, and Florida eventually punted.

A pass interference penalty on Florida cornerback Cormani McClain on the Sooners' ensuing drive gave them 15 yards during a 84-yard drive, ending with a 3-yard touchdown catch by former Gators' tight end Hayden Hansen, a Texas native who transferred to Norman after the 2025 season.

The Gators' run game finally found some traction with Florida trailing 10-0 late in the first half.

After rushing for just 17 yards on the ground in their first three series, the Gators gained 42 of 61 yards on the ground during a 10-play drive, culminating with a 15-yard touchdown scamper by Jadan Baugh. Baugh's 14th rushing touchdown of the season tied him for second all-time at Florida with Emmitt Smith and Buford Long behind Tim Tebow, who had 23 touchdowns on the ground during his 2007 Heisman season.

Oklahoma, though, answered with a 44-yard drive to set up a second field goal by Sandell, the reigning Lou Groza Award winner — this one from 48 yards, to give the Sooners a 13-7 halftime lead.

The Gators' only chance to cut into the lead ended with a head-scratching decision by Sumrall.

A screen pass to Duke Clark, back from a two-game absence with an ankle injury, picked up 33 yards to Oklahoma's 24. After Baugh's 6-yard run on second down, offensive coordinator Buster Faulkner called a quarterback run for the 196-pound Jones, who was stuffed for a 1-yard gain.

Rather than try for a 34-yard field goal, the Gators tried to convert on third down, but Jones underthrew a pass and Courtland Guilroy intercepted it.

The Sooners answered with their epic drive with former Dallas Cowboys' star tight end Jason Witten calling plays for the first time at the college level. Witten, an 11-time Pro Bowler, assumed the offensive coordinator role after coach Brent Venable fired Ben Arbuckle last week.

On a night to celebrate former coach Urban Meyer's induction into the Gators' Ring of Honor, the two-time national championship coach watched his former team turn in a forgettable performance.

#2907. Stats Rundown: 5 numbers from the Mavericks 112-100 Emirates Cup win over the Jazz

The Dallas Mavericks were in a funk for their past two games, with Kyrie Irving sitting one and Cooper Flagg having arguably the worst two-game stretch of his young career. Emirates Cup action raised the intensity and the Mavs stepped up to the tune of a 112-100 win Friday night.

For what felt like the first time in a long time, the Mavs dominated on the boards to the tune of +18 for the night. Dallas knew this was something they needed to work on and they answered the call Friday night. The cherry on top was that rookie Morez Johnson, Jr. led all players 17 boards!

All of those rebounds led to plenty of points for Dallas. They made the most of ending Utah possessions and extending their own possessions, outscoring the Jazz by 15 in second chance points.

The Mavericks got themselves to the line early and often, allowing them to keep the game tight in the first half. Utah was more efficient from the line, missing only a single attempt, but Dallas' 80.6% (25-of-31) was more than enough of an edge, giving the Mavs a +12 advantage from the charity stripe.

The game had an inordinate number of traveling calls; nine to be exact. In the flow of the game, it was quite noticeable and somewhat bizarre. The travel-happy referees were equal opportunity penalizers, handing Utah five of the infractions, and Dallas four of their own.

Dallas opened up a tight game in the third quarter and early part of the fourth. Reeling off a 28-6 scoring run, Dallas extended a one possession game to a 20-point lead. Utah responded with a couple of three-point makes and a smaller run of their own, but that 28-6 run proved to be the deciding factor in a Mavericks' win.

The Mavs needed a win to wash away a bit of the stink from the prior two games and they did all the little things to make it happen against the Jazz. Dallas was locked in on the boards, got to the line, and put together a nice run to build a cushion that proved insurmountable for the Jazz.

#2906. Trump offers UFC chief Dana White press secretary job

President Donald Trump offered UFC president and CEO Dana White the job of press secretary on Saturday.

In a post on Truth Social, Trump said: "I am pleased to announce that the Great Dana White, a violent Fighter and Warrior, has been offered the job of Press Secretary of the United States."

"We need these people to defend us against the Lunatic Left," Trump wrote. "Dana will be a Press Secretary like none other. Dana, please take the job!"

CNN, citing a source, reported White had declined the president's offer.

Earlier Saturday, White posted a picture of himself on Instagram stories standing behind the press secretary's lectern in the White House, with the caption, "On to the next chapter."

It was unclear whether White had accepted the position or was simply acknowledging the president's request as a joke.

Trump said that he was offering White the job of "Press Secretary of the United States," but the president's spokesperson is called the "White House press secretary." Trump also recently speculated about considering Trump loyalist and rapper Nicki Minaj as press secretary, in an interview with podcaster Benny Johnson.

Trump said the job offer to White extended to his assistants, "even though some are not from our Country." The so-called assistants Trump named included current and former UFC mixed martial arts fighters from countries such as Brazil and Russia.

In October, White House press secretary Katie Zacharia left the position just five days after her stint as Karoline Leavitt's replacement began.

The president told reporters he suggested Zacharia quit after her first and only press briefing, where, in her six minutes answering questions behind the lectern, she only took questions from three conservative outlets.

"I just spoke to her about it. I said, 'Katie, is this for you?'" Trump said outside the White House on Oct. 16.

The president's appeal to White follows Friday's announcement that Trump's deputy chief of staff, Stephen Miller, would be promoted to chief of staff, replacing Susie Wiles.

Trump's stunning shake-up came just three days after Democrats won a landslide victory in the midterm elections, gaining majority control in the House and Senate.

The president and White have a longstanding friendship. During Trump's first campaign in 2016, White praised the then-Republican nominee and called him a "fighter."

"Nobody took us seriously. Nobody. Except Donald Trump. Donald was the first guy to recognize the potential we saw in the UFC and encouraged us to build our business," White said.

And for his 80th birthday in June, President Trump hosted a UFC match — dubbed UFC Freedom 250 — on the White House's South Lawn.

The White House and the UFC didn't immediately respond to a request for comment.

— CNBC's Kevin Breuninger contributed to this report.

#2905. Anthropic IPO meets AI backlash after midterms show stinging rebuke of data centers

Americans were unequivocal at the ballot box this week as they expressed disdain for tech billionaires and mistrust of artificial intelligence. With that backdrop, Anthropic is about to try to go public.

The AI company is gearing up for a potentially historic IPO, likely targeting mid-November for its Nasdaq debut. CNBC reported on Friday that Anthropic investors see the company aiming for a valuation of $1 trillion to $1.5 trillion, down from a target of $2 trillion that had been floated in recent months.

From Wall Street to Main Street, it's becoming a tougher environment for Anthropic. The Nasdaq on Thursday suffered its steepest drop in 19 months, and it's down to start November after October marked its worst month since March 2025. Chipmakers and neoclouds, the companies most closely tied to the AI buildout, are getting hit the hardest.

Voters rebuked AI in Tuesday's midterm elections, while sending Democrats to sweeping congressional victories across the country. Candidates who advocated for more stringent AI regulation won handily in states like Michigan, Ohio, Texas and Georgia, which will all now have Democratic governors, and local communities opposed data center-related ballot measures in 40 out of 41 cases.

More than half of voters said AI data centers were a "crucial" or "major factor" in who they supported for Congress, according to an exit poll from NBC News.

"People don't want a data center going near them if it's just going to house AI that's going to help a big tech company profit," Maura Austin, a behavioral scientist at the University of Virginia, told CNBC. "What Anthropic is doing when they're going public with such a high valuation is they're really leaning into that big tech, billionaire kind of caricature. I expect that's not going to go well with the public."

Anthropic has long tried to position itself as a safety-conscious company that takes AI's potential risks and benefits seriously. CEO Dario Amodei co-founded the company alongside a handful of researchers who defected from OpenAI, now its chief rival. Anthropic is best known for developing a family of AI models called Claude, and it primarily sells its products and services to other businesses.

The company's enterprise focus has helped it avoid becoming the public face of AI, leaving that distinction to OpenAI and CEO Sam Altman. But with Anthropic poised to become the first pure-play AI company to hit the public market, minting a number of new billionaires and many more millionaires in the process, a heightened level of scrutiny is coming.

"Being a public company, just that by itself pushes you in the limelight," Reena Aggarwal, a professor of finance at Georgetown University, said in an interview. "And then AI is such a hot-button issue that Anthropic will be in the limelight even more."

Matt Kennedy, a senior IPO market strategist at Renaissance Capital, said that's not necessarily a bad thing for Anthropic.

Public markets force greater transparency, he said, and the company could attract new users through its debut. And since AI has become such a mainstream conversation topic, several private companies are already household names.

"I would say that a public company does not deserve more criticisms than a comparable private company," Kennedy said. "So Anthropic should not be put in a harsher public spotlight than OpenAI just because it's public."

AI backlash accelerated over the summer after a series of high-profile safety and security incidents rattled the public. As concerns mounted, the debate over data centers intensified, while tech's hyperscalers simultaneously boosted their forecasts for capital expenditures, each reaching into the hundreds of billions of dollars.

Anthropic and OpenAI depend on state-of-the-art data centers to train models and serve customers, and they're trying to build out capacity as quickly as possible to meet demand. Anthropic said in an amended IPO prospectus this week that it has entered into purchase commitments and lease agreements with an aggregate committed spend of about $895.4 billion, "substantially all of which is related to compute and infrastructure."

Americans grappling with inflation and stubbornly high gas prices have grown worried that the facilities could exacerbate environmental harms, raise utility bills and contribute to job loss. For many, a proposed data center in their backyard became the physical manifestation of the widespread antipathy toward AI.

In the risk factors section of its prospectus, Anthropic said opposition to data center development has become more organized across jurisdictions, and that the company's public profile could draw particular scrutiny to projects associated with it.

Nicole Greene, an analyst at Gartner, said she expects that Anthropic's executives have long been prepared to contend with AI backlash, especially given the disruptive nature of the technology.

"There's going to be big emotions," Greene told CNBC. "With big emotions and feelings — fear and excitement — comes heightened scrutiny."

Amodei acknowledged the souring sentiment around AI in a post on X in August. He said it's the result of a "crisis of trust," and that people believe companies, the government and the tech industry are always "cooking up some new way to screw them over."

Rebuilding public confidence is going to be an uphill battle as the company begins providing quarterly financial updates.

The University of Southern California published a survey on Monday that found that 71% of respondents don't trust big tech companies to act in society's best interest "at all" around data center development.

"Once you've lost trust, it's hard to gain it back," Wändi Bruine de Bruin, a professor at the University of Southern California's Schaeffer Institute, said in an interview. "The numbers are not good."

#2904. How Patriots legend Rob Gronkowski truly felt about Tom Brady’s presence at his New England Hall of Fame ceremony

How Patriots legend Rob Gronkowski truly felt about Tom Brady's presence at his New England Hall of Fame ceremony appeared first on ClutchPoints. Add ClutchPoints as a Preferred Source by clicking here.

Rob Gronkowski had plenty of familiar faces in his corner for his New England Patriots Hall of Fame induction on Saturday, but one presence clearly stood out above the rest. The former tight end opened up about what it meant to have Tom Brady by his side for the big day.

"It meant everything… He wanted to be here. It just meant a lot, man. Tom can be anywhere else. He's the greatest player of all time. It just meant a lot to myself, my family, and to the fans, I'm sure as well," Gronkowski said, via Justin Turpin.

Brady received a loud ovation from the crowd at Patriot Place Plaza, where many of Gronkowski's former teammates and coaches gathered for the ceremony. The legendary quarterback also showed his former tight end some love on social media, reposting a video of the two embracing with a simple message.

The mutual admiration makes sense. Brady and Gronkowski formed one of the most dominant quarterback-tight end duos in NFL history, winning three Super Bowls together in New England before reuniting with the Tampa Bay Buccaneers to capture a fourth title in the 2020 season.

Gronkowski was voted into the Patriots Hall of Fame by fans in April, in his first year of eligibility. He joined Brady, who was inducted in 2024.

The honor caps a remarkable career in New England. Gronkowski spent nine seasons with the Patriots after being selected in the second round of the 2010 NFL Draft out of Arizona. He racked up 521 receptions for 7,861 yards and 79 touchdowns in the regular season, and his 80 total touchdowns rank first in franchise history. He was also a five-time Pro Bowler and four-time First-Team All-Pro.

Patriots owner Robert Kraft was unable to attend the ceremony while recovering from back surgery, but he had high praise for Gronkowski when the honor was announced.

"Rob became a fan favorite almost immediately and remained the standard at tight end for nearly a decade," Kraft said.

The celebration continues on Sunday, when Gronkowski will be honored at halftime of the Patriots' game against the Green Bay Packers. Brady will be on hand for that one as well, calling the game from the FOX broadcast booth.

#2903. The AI ‘credit card’ is getting costlier as CoreWeave bond yield tops 16%

Debt markets are sending a message to AI data center builders: we're hiking the interest rate on your credit card and may put some new curbs on your credit limit.

That analogy comes courtesy of Jefferies global head of equity strategy, Christopher Wood, who put it in a note on Friday: "The most plausible way the AI capex arms race will end is that the credit card will be taken away."

That isn't happening yet, but credit markets have been spooked by the new political landscape — seeing more hurdles to data centers under Democratic governors in key states, along with a Democratic-controlled Congress.

The poster child for debt stress is neocloud Coreweave. The yield on its 2032 bond with a 9.625% coupon has climbed above 16%. Coreweave's five-year credit default swaps spiked above 1,289 basis points on Thursday, widening above their October peak, according to Deutsche Bank. The CDS eased 8% Friday to around 1,155.

"We've had plenty of warnings from the credit space," said Jim Reid, Deutsche Bank's global head of macro and thematic research, in a note on Friday. Debt and equities are now moving more in sync, he added, "so we'll have to see if credit goes any further."

Investors are demanding more compensation to insure against default among even companies with the strongest balance sheets and credit ratings, including Nvidia, Broadcom, and Amazon.com.

CDS on Nvidia debt is up 76% in the last month. Broadcom's CDS is up 49%, while Amazon's has gained 44%. Their CDS remain low on an absolute basis; Nvidia's spread stood at roughly 139 basis points, compared with about 298 bps for Oracle and roughly 1,100 for CoreWeave. A large increase from a low starting point does not imply the same credit risk.

Still, rising CDS are a sign that markets want more protection against default among even the largest and healthiest debt issuers. And rising CDS imply higher financing costs down the line.

The market is now grappling with the prospect that at least some data center spending will be delayed or scrapped under incoming Democratic governors in Ohio, Texas, and Georgia, the three states with much of the planned capacity.

Moratoriums could impact 45% of planned capacity in the U.S. with a large amount of that in Texas, according to Goldman Sachs. Texas governor-elect Gina Hinojosa has said she's not opposed to data centers but wants a moratorium until the state legislature passes protections for communities.

None of these means AI debt financing is collapsing. Goldman tallies five AI bond deals totaling $67 billion since October 29, pushing total hyperscaler debt issuance this year to $296 billion. Goldman forecasts $440 billion of hyperscaler debt issuance next year, plus an additional $300 billion of project-level issuance across investment-grade and high-yield.

The Trump administration is vowing to support data centers. National Economic Council Director Kevin Hassett told CNBC on Friday that he expects data center growth to continue as forecast, despite Democrats' big election wins.

The big question now is how quickly AI spending will generate enough cash for hyperscalers to keep investors comfortable with future capex plans. For the neoclouds, any sign of consolidation or retrenchment in capex could result in fewer or shorter contracts for data center compute or delays in prepayments.

Goldman analysts said hyperscalers collectively reported negative free cash flow in the third quarter as capital spending exceeded operating cash flow.

"Delayed monetization and growth in purchase commitments—which we view as additional and sizable uses of cash, separate from Capex—are the key risks to more debt issuance, subject to market conditions," according to the bank.

Peak AI debt issuance may now be closer at hand. Bank of America analysts estimates that AI-related credit issuance will rise 31% from 2026 to 2027, hitting $685 billion, including public and private debt. They then see it falling 23% to $531 billion in 2028.

As for the risk? "Downside to these forecasts exists from a potential slowdown in AI infrastructure projects on the back of the Blue Wave, which will reduce capex needs and thus debt market funding," BofA said in a note.

-CNBC's Nick Wells and Michael Bloom contributed reporting

#2902. Luka Doncic is soaring, Lakers are No. 1 in West. His brilliance is leaving mark

He had recently likened Luka Dončić to a supernova. He said you have to pick your poison against him.

So, when asked if he had any superlatives to offer following Dončić's 53-point, five-rebound, five-assist performance in the Lakers' 124-118 win over the Trail Blazers on Friday, Redick stumbled.

When a reporter started asking another question, he interjected.

"I'm going to just sit around, we'll gather one day and come up with more superlatives," Redick added. "So I'll have more words ready. But for now, I don't have any words."

The hyper-prepared Lakers coach was finally ill-prepared for an assignment.

Redick isn't the only person who has been left dumbfounded following Dončić's blazing start to the season in which he's averaging a league-leading 40.1 points, 7.7 rebounds and 7.6 assists.

In Friday's game, Dončić passed Michael Jordan (347) for the most points scored in the firstnine games of a season since the NBA-ABA merger with 361 points.

Los Angeles Lakers guard Luka Doncic shoots a free throw during an NBA basketball game against the Portland Trail Blazers in Los Angeles, Friday, Nov. 6, 2026. AP Photo/Mark J. Terrill

It's the most points anyone has scored in the modern NBA since Wilt Chamberlain in 1962-63.

"I'm having a lot of fun," Dončić said. "Obviously playing basketball, it's a lot of fun for me. It's more [fun] when you play like this and when you win games. So, it's been like this my whole life. Basketball was my dream. Playing in the NBA was my dream. Just living my dream."

The 27-year-old face of their franchise is having an MVP-caliber campaign while leading the Lakers to the No. 1 seed in the Western Conference with an 8-2 record.

No one expected the Lakers to be this good.

And no one expected Dončić to soar to the top of the MVP conversation after he hadn't finished in the top two in voting ever in his career.

Los Angeles Lakers guard Luka Doncic, left, drives to the basket against Portland Trail Blazers center Robert Williams III on Friday, Nov. 6, 2026, in Los Angeles. AP Photo/Mark J. Terrill

But if the first few weeks of the season have made anything clear, it's that Redick needs to beef up his vocabulary. He needs to get a thesaurus. Dončić is skyrocketing, and he's carrying the Lakers with him.

There were many incredible moments in the Lakers' 16-point come-from-behind win over the Trail Blazers in which they stormed back after shooting 4-for-23 from beyond the arc in the first half, including missing all 15 of their initial attempts from that distance.

There was the buzzer-beating 62-foot shot that Dončić made at the end of the second quarter, a shot he literally banked in from three-quarters of the court.

"Luka can do some things that maybe a handful, if not no one, can do in basketball," Redick said.

Los Angeles Lakers guard Luka Doncic, left, shoots over Portland Trail Blazers center Robert Williams III on Friday, Nov. 6, 2026, in Los Angeles. AP Photo/Mark J. Terrill

There was a little mixup after Dončić notched his 11th 50-point performance in his career, surpassing Kareem Abdul-Jabbar for 10th-most such games in NBA history.

It all started because Ziaire Williams couldn't wait to congratulate Dončić.

So much so that Williams decided to interrupt what he thought was Dončić's walk-off television interview with "NBA on Prime" by approaching him from behind, putting his hands on his shoulders and yelling, "Fifty ball."

Austin Reaves was doing the interview. Not Dončić.

"I'm the wrong white person," said Reaves, who had 25 points.

Did Williams seriously confuse his two teammates? "Deadass," he said, flashing a smile in the Lakers' locker room.

"That was very disrespectful to me," Dončić said jokingly of being confused with Reaves, with whom he shares a brotherly bond.

It has been a wild stretch for Dončić, who's shattering records nightly.

He's leaving his coach at a loss for words. He's making shots from outer space. And he's leaving his teammates seeing his face in everyone.

"I feel like that," he said. "But as long as we win, I'm good."

#2898. VfB Stuttgart vs. Werder Bremen preview: Undav and Füllkrug in contrasting form ahead of Topspiel

Deniz Undav's VfB Stuttgart will host Niclas Füllkrug's Werder Bremen in the marquee fixture of matchday 10.

The captain's goal drought contributed to Stuttgart's slow start to the season. Undav had found the net twice, but none of those goals came in the league as his poor form led him to miss out on Jürgen Klopp's squad nomination for the second time.

Meanwhile, Ermedin Demirović has been their go-to guy this season, although he was unable to score in the defeat against Galatasaray, who had an unsuccessful attempt to sign the Bosnian striker in the summer. Stuttgart returned to winning ways in Bundesliga on matchday 8 after successive defeats.

The absence of Atakan Karazor and Angelo Stiller in midfield would cause selection issues for Hoeneß, although the former has a slight chance to recover from an injury that forced his early substitution against Galatasaray.

Demirović and Undav started together against both Leverkusen and the Turkish champions, although Hoeneß preferred different playing systems. In goal, Dennis Seimen is expected to return in place of the experienced Fabian Bredlow.

After matching Rudi Völler's club record of scoring in seven successive Bundesliga games, Füllkrug earned a national team call-up by Klopp on Thursday. The 33-year-old leads the scoring chart in Bundesliga, and he already opened a five-goal lead over a certain Harry Kane.

The nine goals from the former Torjägerkanone winner helped Werder Bremen to start the matchday in fifth place after having to fight relegation consistently in past seasons. Daniel Thioune’s troops are unbeaten in Bundesliga since starting the campaign with a 4-1 defeat to Freiburg.

Mitchell Weiser suffered another injury setback inside the first 15 minutes in last week's 2-0 win over TSG Hoffenheim, and he'll miss this one at the very least. Eren Dinkci, who scored the second goal after replacing him, is expected to start in the right wing.

Werder Bremen are unbeaten in their last two trips to Stuttgart, although the Swabians did win 4-0 at Weserstadion last season. The last time a game between the two sides ended goalless was in February 2006.

#2897. Den Questions: Finding Identity In Turmoil

Welcome back to the Den. The Wolves are ten games in and their record stands at 7-3. Over that time the Wolves sprinted out of the gates, lost Anthony Edwards to dirty knees in need of a cleaning, and have mostly righted the ship. Their losses are on travel back-to-backs and their first game without Edwards where they didn't even have a chance to practice before tip-off.

This team found something special in their first four games and then had their literal knees cut from them. Through the mire of the early season, as the rest of the league reels from their own injuries, the Wolves are looking for another new identity. An identity that does not include Edwards but prepares for his return.

As you can imagine, there are a lot of questions for this team to answer. Here are some of them.

Note: So much of this is small sample size theater.

It's just flashes. He had similar highs in Golden State before cooling down. There's no way he can keep this up. Here he goes again driving into traffic to get blocked… huh, nice pass. No no no, don't shoot tha…SPLASH! Oh my gawd, he got up so high for that rebound.

This short-staged drama has occurred inside my head in nearly every game this year. In good games and bad, Jonathan Kuminga has shown up to do whatever is asked of him.

In every counting stat except points per game, Kuminga is averaging career bests; rebounds (5.7), assists (2.9), steals (1.2) and blocks (1.0). His highest BPM through his first five seasons was 0.1. This season he is at 2.9 and has his career first positive for Offensive Box Plus Minus (0.6).

Head Coach Chris Finch has heaped praise on JK. In Minnesota's more read and react, flow, style, the Wolves starting power forward is emboldened to make a play when the ball finds him. His cuts aren't always rewarded but it sends the defense into a panic and opens things up for his teammates. It also puts him in the middle of the floor to grab or poke out offensive rebounds.

I am running out of excuses for why it won't last. After the first few games, I thought his play would disappear once the initial novelty of the season's start wore off. It didn't.

I thought he'd become more shot hungry and ball dominant when Ant went down. He hasn't.

I thought he'd get worn down having to guard some of the best players in the league night after night. This might happen as the overlong season grinds on, but so far Kuminga seems to get up for the battle.

The hardest question might be coming, "Can the Wolves afford to keep Kuminga?"

The start of the season has seen a dramatic drop in Rudy Gobert's points per game and his near league-best shooting efficiency. Gobert is averaging his fourth fewest shot attempts, second least points per game, the worst shooting percentage, and worst free throw percentage of his career.

It took one of the smartest basketball minds in Mike Conley over a year to learn how to play with Rudy. LaMelo Ball has had a scant few weeks to build chemistry. Combine that with the geometry of the floor and the defensive attention he is getting due to the sidelined Edwards, and it's no surprise the lack of consistency.

The Wolves need Rudy to be effective on offense. The book is out on the big man. Good defenses know how to bait Minnesota into thinking Rudy is open and recover quick enough to force a turnover or an errant shot.

Which brings us to the question of how to effectively use Gobert on offense. There is space to use him in the pick n' roll, but that has rarely been Finch's play style of choice. He is averaging a career high in offensive rebounds and this is primarily where I want him to find his points.

Finishing those misses just as they bounce off or tipping them out to teammates to try again when defenders are too close to him. Don't give them a chance to send him to the line.

He is very good at screening his teammates open and should continue to be used as the effective body in the way. This opens up Ball, Ayo Dosunmu, and Bones Hyland to get a screen assist into open triples. But this also pulls Rudy away from the hoop and the offensive boards that are his best offense.

Simple as it sounds his threat to roll is only as effective as his chemistry with his point guard. Until that firms up, he's on cleanup duty.

The Wolves are getting spooky with a lot more ghost screens this season. This gambit, that asks a shooting threat to feint a screen before sprinting to the other side of the point-of-attack (POA) defender, is meant to short circuit a defense. In a fraction of a second, the ghost screener's defender has to determine if they need to call for a switch if the screen gets set, or to chase after the shooter who has suddenly burst to a preferred place to spot up.

The Wolves are loaded with players who can quickly turn on the jets (I can't recommend Ayo Dosumnu's Player's Tribune piece enough) and make this decision that much harder. Screens are overcome best when a defense is communicating. The threat of a ghost screen shortens that window for effective communication.

If the POA defender thinks a screen is about to be set, they might get ready to hedge for the incoming contact giving the ball handler enough wiggle room to drive. If the chase defender thinks a screen is being set, they might slow up enough to give the ghost screener a chance to find an open look. If a screen is set, the game flows into common pick n' roll, one of the most tried and true means of creating advantages.

In the clip below, the Wolves use a combination of screens to break Orlando's zone, a past bugaboo for Minnesota.

Armed with better outside shooting than the past two years, look for the Wolves to continue to haunt perimeter defenders with these scary non-screens.

Note: Every injury to a player is unique. No timeline to return or level of ability when returning is the same. An Achilles tear for Donte DiVincenzo and his play style is a completely different injury that an Achilles tear for Ariel Hukporti and how he approaches the game, despite hurting the same tendon.

The day after it was announced that Ant would get arthroscopic knee surgery, a friend of mine sent me a grim text. All he could think of was Grant Hill, Brandon Roy, and Penny Hardaway and the possibility that Ant's career would be sapped from him like it had from those elite athletes.

I immediately texted back "What about DWade, Kobe, CP3?" After a brief internet search here are several NBA players who have undergone arthroscopic knee surgery who had some of their best years after the fact.

Kobe Bryant – Kobe had his first knee surgery in 2003, another in 2006, and another in 2010. In the midst of this string of surgeries he was an All-Star every year (despite the Laker bump, these were earned), won an MVP and was top five in MVP voting in all but one year, and made All-Defensive Teams in every year but one.

Dwyane Wade – DWade (a frequent Ant comp) had his entire left meniscus removed in 2002 while in college and went on to have a Hall of Fame career where he was unwillingly nicknamed Flash for how quick he was despite playing sans knee cartilage.

Chris Paul – Snitch Paul had arthroscopic knee surgery to repair a meniscus tear in 2010 while still with the New Orleans Hornets. As fun as he was on those Hornets teams, the Point God emerged into a force in the years after with the Lob City Clippers, Hardenball Rockets, and even made his first Finals with the Phoenix Suns, 11 years after the surgery.

Jimmy Butler – General Soreness had surgery on his right knee in 2018 while the Wolves were third in the Western Conference. He returned to help break the 14 year playoff drought. Jimmy has since been the driving force to two NBA finalist teams, never winning one. He adds multiple All-Star selection, an All-NBA selection, and an All-Defensive Team honor to his otherwise championship barren trophy case. I'm not bitter.

Jaylen Brown – Mr. Lamb Chops and Gravy had a knee procedure in the summer of 2025 and was back by training camp en route to being an All-Star, All-NBA 2nd Team, and sixth in MVP voting.

This surgery to clean up Ant's knee will likely diminish Ant's burst athleticism. He has moved his game to more outside shooting while maintaining a rim presence. If his downhill game is not as quick, it will hurt, but he's also added a midrange for if the defender goes too far under that leaves him a three level problem for any defense.

Plenty of NBA greats have bounced back, here's hoping Ant joins this laudable list.

Ant is out for roughly two months. Terrance Shannon Jr. has missed two games with a hip contusion. Donte DiVincenzo is recovering from an Achilles tear with every intention of coming back this season.

In their absence the Wolves have slotted Ayo Dosunmu into the starting lineup. Bones Hyland is scoring the ball well. Jaylen Clark is getting into guys jerseys and hitting 41.2% of his threes on 2.1 attempts a game.

All of these players are competing with each other for minutes. While the deeper bench has done yeomen's work to keep the Wolves head above water, they are not as good as the players whose minutes they are sopping up.

Finch is strict in his rotations. In the last three years, his starting lineups are in the top five for most used lineups in the NBA. If he gets a fully healthy team, Bones and Clark's minutes will likely disappear all together. Ayo will move back into his sixth man role ready to be whatever his team needs of him.

Donte is the biggest question mark. He started all 82 games last season, but there is not a spot for him in this starting lineup. We also don't know what he will look like coming back from such a devastating injury.

Sans LaMelo foul trouble giving Bones more minutes in the Raptors game, Finch has only given double digit minutes out to nine players before the injuries started. Joan Beringer's minutes aren't going anywhere. Trey Lyles has size, outside shooting, and has shown a predilection for being in the right place at the right time. Unless countering a specific lineup, his minutes stay the same.

Shannon figures to be the first cut in playtime with the Big Ragu's return unless Finch expands his bench to ten. And yet, there are few players who could elevate TJ's game better than Donte. An attacking TJ off a Beringer screen with Donte, Ayo, and Trey spreading the floor is tailormade to confound defenses.

On those nights where the injury report is blank, I hope Finch keeps the pedal to the metal with frequent rotations in the vein of Memphis Grizzlies coach Tuomas Iisalo. The Grizz have 13 players averaging double digit minutes and only two players over 30 minutes a game, Cameron Boozer (32.3) and Ty Jerome (30.7).

The NBA season is grueling. We have seen year after year the Wolves struggle as the playoffs wear on. TJ, Bones, and Clark are good players, deserving of minutes that will keep the starters fresh for when they are needed most.

Look here for the inarguable answers through the first ten games.

The Wolves are presently top five in the league in the following team categories:

The Net Rating stat is leaning heavily on Minnesota's first three blow out wins. That said, they have only lost their games by a combined 17 points thus far. They are hitting plenty of threes. They are defending with energy and diving for loose balls. They don't treat the game as something that should be handed to them like some legacy Ivy Leaguer applying at his dad's company. They are battling for the ball and snatching it from less hungry competition.

Gone are the days of XL T-Shirts and Howl Towels. The Wolves have overloaded this season with all manner of tchotchke, curio and baseball jersey to lure fans and collectors alike to home games. Most of these do not have an image released but give you just enough information to send your imagination whirring.

There is an entire series of baseball hats with design help from former Wolves vets. For Kevin Garnett's jersey retirement, Minnesota has five unique pieces of career spanning collectible celebrating the Big Ticket's accomplishments wearing 21. There are bobbleheads, blankets, and backpacks and way way way too many baseball jerseys. And here they all are, definitively ranked.

There are seven ticket package giveaways featuring a baseball jersey this season. In collaboration with local artists, this is what the Wolves are doing for their Artist's Series this year. Each jersey, for a sport different than the one it is honoring, celebrates a societal cross-section; Latino Noche, Military Appreciation, Native American Heritage, Pride Night, Asian American and Pacific Islander Night, Black Excellence, and Inspiring Women.

I think the designs are fun. Each one is a variation with pin stripes in a variety of colors. Some of them have designs on the sleeve nodding to the theme of the night. All of them have "Wolves Basketball" on the back. And all of them are baseball jerseys.

I love the Artist's series. I have purchased at least one shirt each of the last two seasons. I love that a local artist gets to put their stamp on the Wolves design. I love that several of the local artists are BIPOC and women creators. I love that they came in a variety of T-shirts, sweatshirts, and hats.

I just don't understand the thought process in making seven different baseball jerseys. I don't know enough about production costs, or textiles, so forgive my potentially ignorant question, but, wouldn't a jersey with two less sleeves of material be cheaper while closer to the topic at hand? Can they not do basketball jerseys because of some contractual stuff with Nike? WHY!?

All that said, I think the Native American design is my favorite and I might need to get one.

The Wolves have played ten games in 17 days. If losing Ant and TJ wasn't enough, they have not had more than a day between games and twice have played in travel back-to-backs. All marathons are tough. Some like to remind you of that fact in the first few miles.

The giddy win streak to start the season, like those first marathon steps past gathered supporters, can make you feel invincible. Before you know it, you're running faster than you can sustain and its only mile two.

This is not to say that what we saw in the first three games is unsustainable, but with Ant out, the team is grasping for an identity while still playing fast and loose. LaMelo is trying to play aggressive defense and fouling like crazy.

The race is still young though. The stored ATP that let the Wolves sprint out of the gate may be spent, but they have years of experience and the benefits of youth. In the first full month of the season, they are searching for a pace they can maintain.

The challenge of racing a marathon is knowing the line between speed and overheating. The Wolves need to keep chasing after rebounds and through screens without losing anyone else to injury. If the news of Donte's return holds true, and Ant and TJ bounce back, they'll get an injection of energy better than any banana or unsavory gel packet found along the march.

From now until the day after Thanksgiving (no it is not a holiday and you can't make me recognize it as one) the Wolves take on a mix of the top and bottom of both conferences. They play nine of them at home. Here's a snap shot of who they got.

The promise that the Utah Jazz were going to try this year has landed on its face due to injury and their inability to defend the point of attack. The Bulls are finally rebuilding. The Nets have five players in their sophomore season and only two of them get playing time despite their abysmal record.

On the challenging side of the opposition, Cooper Flagg looks incredible alongside the returned Kyrie Irving. The Mavericks are a deep squad and can hang around in any game. The Memphis Grizzlies, on the back of Ty Jerome's electric start and the connective play of rookie Cam Boozer are near the top of the conference.

Portland Trail Blazer's head coach Micah Nori returns to Minnesota for a home back-to-back. Nori has his team cooking, bolstered by Blazer legend, the renewed Damian Lillard.

The Knicks have started off shaky but can find their championship form on any given night. Finally, the Thunder are a battered and broken team that still boasts seven players that could earn starter's minutes on most teams in the league.

With the Wolves only having one back to back in this stretch, and three days of rest between game 11 and 12 to rest and practice, I see the Wolves going into the month of December playing at the same pace, 14-6.

#2893. JJ Redick was pleased with ‘awesome’ season debut from Adou Thiero

One of the standouts of the early stages of the preseason was Adou Thiero, who looked to have built on a strong Summer League and was ready to be part of the Lakers' rotation.

However, an ill-timed injury halted Thiero's progress and kept him out of the opening weeks of the regular season. After some patience to allow swelling in his knee to go down, Thiero finally made his season debut in Friday's win over the Blazers and looked like he hadn't missed a beat from the preseason.

In only six minutes, Thiero's impact was felt as he tallied four points, four rebounds and two assists. After the game, head coach JJ Redick was asked about his performance and got rave reviews.

"I thought his first two defensive possessions, he made mistakes and then after that he was awesome," Redick said. "He literally did everything we asked him to do. He was great on the offensive glass. Even sometimes when we didn't necessarily get the possession, he was tapping the ball and crashing.

"I thought the read he made out of the short roll, I think they blitzed three times in the first half and two of those were against him. He makes the short roll and he gets the lob. I thought he played a great game and, defensively, he was really good in the second half."

Thiero came in late in the first quarter in the role previously held by Jarred Vanderbilt and immediately made an impact. Given his leaping ability, Thiero being the ball screener and roll man felt like an obvious fit.

The Blazers immediately tested him as they blitzed Luka Dončić, forcing Thiero to not just be in the right spot in the short roll, but then make a decision. He nailed both aspects, setting up Ziaire Williams for a dunk.

Shortly after, the Blazers opted for a more traditional pick-and-roll coverage. This time, Thiero rolled hard to the rim and caught the lob for an athletic finish.

"He did great," Walker Kessler said of Thiero. "Obviously, we value Adou a lot. He's a very physical player. Really talented, athletic player. I mean, that lob he caught is very, very impressive. He had a really good debut for the season."

Thiero's athleticism is unparalleled on the roster — and among the elites of the elite in the league as a whole — and offers the Lakers a different dynamic. Thiero as a screener and roller won't always work as it did on Friday, but his athleticism and activity level will continue to jump off the screen.

The Lakers' hope now is that he can stay healthy and start to stack positive performances in the regular season.

#2892. St. Cloud State surrenders five points to Omaha as McLaughlin carted off ice

ST. CLOUD —The St. Cloud State men's hockey team faltered on the road against Omaha, going 0-1-1 this weekend to fall to 3-5-1 overall.

In the first game, the Huskies battled back from a one-goal deficit to earn a 2-2 tie against the Mavericks on Friday night at Baxter Arena, but Omaha claimed the NCHC standings points with a 1-0 victory in a shootout following a scoreless overtime period.

The Huskies struck early on the power play when freshman defenseman Will Sharpe scored his first collegiate goal just 1:49 into the contest. Omaha swiftly answered on a goal from Gavin Hodnett before Ryan McCleary paced the Mavs ahead in the second period.

St. Cloud State responded in the third period after generating sustained pressure and capitalizing on another power-play opportunity. Following a 5-on-3 advantage, freshman forward Hayden Paupanekis generated a scoring opportunity, leading to sophomore defenseman Finn McLaughlin burying the tying goal into an open net. The Huskies looked much more polished in the final period, outshooting Omaha 17-6, but could not find the go-ahead goal.

In the second game of the road trip, the Huskies continued their power play barrage with three additional PP scores from William Ahlrik, Nolan Roed and Carson Pilgrim, but could not keep up with the Mavs in a 5-3 loss, surrendering five points total for the weekend.

#2890. Early-season check-in on new Rockets Marcus Smart and Bogdan Bogdanovic

With the season being roughly one-tenth of the way through, I figured we could do a check-in on the two of the major offseason acquisitions, Bogdan Bogdanovic and Marcus Smart, who were signed to contracts paying them $3,524,115 and $6,064,00 this season, respectively.

Marcus Smart brings a lot more to the table for the Rockets than what appears in the box score. His defensive abilities, constant energy, and ability to make opposing guards miserable earned him Defensive Player of the Year honors in 2022. He brings another veteran presence to a young Rockets locker room. He will not be expected to score each night, but his ball-handling, shot creation, and the ability to make timely shots will make him a key piece of the Rockets' rotation.

So far this season, Marcus is averaging 9.8 points, 2.5 assists, 2.8 rebounds, and 1.3 steals while shooting 38.7% from three and 44.2% from the field. The field goal percentage is trending higher than his career average, as it three-point shooting, which is incredibly encouraging. Smart also had a stellar game vs. the Thunder, where he scored 24 points and made 7 three-pointers.

Houston needs perimeter shooting, and the 38% three-point shooter for his entire NBA career provides that. Bogdanovic can also create off the dribble at the second level of the offense and make shots when the main offensive players are on the bench or under heavy defensive pressure. He can create off the dribble at the second level of the offense and can make shots when the main offensive players are on the bench or under heavy pressure from the defense. This season, Bogdanovic is averaging 5.6 points per game, which does not seem like a lot, but he is shooting a blistering 45.5% from three. Any team can use that.

Bogdanovic is an excellent attacking addition to this year's sqaud. He can shoot, create off the dribble and space the floor, so he meets many of the Rockets' needs. However, his health and consistency will determine the effectiveness of this signing.

What is interesting about both of these acquisitions is the varying amounts of impact that each can bring to a game. Smart is brought in for his defense, energy, and leadership. Bogdanovic brings shooting, creation on offense, and floor spacing. Both players bring experience to Houston's young core and can help ease the transition of some of the young players into more rotation minutes as they continue to grow. They can be of use to players like Reed Sheppard by having veteran players on the team to help support the young players and to allow for more flexibility with lineups.

Ultimately, the value of both of these players will be determined by how they perform in games of consequence. Houston is trying to build a team of players that can help get them past the first round in the postseason, not just a collection of veterans and youngsters. If I had to give a grade for the two signings so far, I would give the Bogdonovic signing an A, and the Marcus Smart signing an A- only because Smart has missed time with an injury already.

Thats all for today! Make sure to let me know what you all think about the signings in the comments, and as always make sure to check back at The Dream Shake for all things Houston Rockets coverage.

#2888. Giants add former third-round pick Jalin Hyatt to 53-man roster

The New York Giants on Saturday signed wide receiver Jalin Hyatt off their practice squad to the 53-man roster, filling the spot vacated when they released quarterback Jake Haener earlier in the week.

The Giants traded up in the 2023 NFL Draft to select Hyatt in the third round, and he has had a disappointing career to this point. In three seasons, Hyatt has caught only 36 passes. Over the past two seasons, that total is just 13.

New head coach John Harbaugh gave Hyatt an opportunity, however, similar to the one he gave to Deonte Banks and other previously disappointing Giants draft picks.

Hyatt, despite an injury that cost him time during training camp, was impressive enough in the summer to earn a spot on the practice squad.

Hyatt has played in just one game for the Giants this season, and has no catches.

With wide receiver/kick returner Charlie Jones ailing with a hand injury, Hyatt provides depth at receiver and could fill in as a kickoff returner. Hyatt averaged 27.8 yards on four kickoff returns last season.

With Hyatt moving to the 53-man roster, the Giants re-signed Haener to their practice squad.

The Giants elevated two players from their practice squad for Sunday's game against the Philadelphia Eagles — running back Phil Mafah and defensive end Dawuane Smoot.

With Tyrone Tracy on IR and Cam Skattebo doubtful with a hamstring injury, the Giants have only Devin Singletary and Gregory Desrosiers as healthy backs on their active roster.

Mafah, 6-foot-1, 232 pounds, was a seventh-round selection by the Dallas Cowboys in the 2025 NFL Draft. He played in just one game, getting five carries for 18 yards and a touchdown. Mafah signed to the practice squad in mid-September after being waived by Dallas.

Smoot, 31, is in his 10th NFL season. The 6-foot-3, 264-pound 31-year-old was signed to the practice squad in mid-October.

#2886. 5 questions with Cat Scratch Reader

This week I had a chance to exchange questions with Walker Clement of SBNation's Cat Scratch Reader about our upcoming matchup. This is no SB50 rematch but there are a lot of good storylines to tackle (see what I did there?)

The irony is that Walker now lives in Colorado and I'm in the Carolinas…what are the odds? He had fantastic answers to my questions so enjoy this extra insight into the 2026 Panthers (and thank him in the comments while convincing him to become at least a secondary Broncos fan!)

MHR – Carolina has swung wildly this year with wins of 34-3 at Atlanta and 25-14 at Philadelphia, then a 43-13 loss to Tampa Bay. Which version of the Panthers can Denver expect Sunday?

WC: Smart money is on the "vs Tampa Bay" version of the Panthers for a number of reasons, but the only real important one is the health of their current roster. The Carolina Panthers are currently without their RT1, LT1, LG1, CB1, CB2, CB3, CB4, DT3, OLB 1, OLB 2, ILB 2, RB2, RB3, and WR3. Two of those guys missed last week's game against the Packers, the rest have been missing since sometime between September and January. The depth chart is rough right now. Of the guys who are playing, their current starting left tackle (Rasheed Walker), center (Luke Fortner), and right guard (Robert Hunt) are each leading the league in individual pressures allowed from their respective positions. It is no accident that Bryce Young was sacked 11 times in five days last week. Fixing the protection is a priority for them right now. The progress they have made since last Thursday could well decide the game this week.

MHR – Bryce Young has completed 59-percent of his passes, thrown for 1,921 yards, 13 touchdowns, and just 5 interceptions on the season. He is doing well on first down and is moving the chains early and often, spreading the ball around. As Denver can be known for giving up big plays, which receiving threats should scare Denver's DBs the most?

WC: Reigning Offensive Rookie of the Year Tetairoa McMillan is your obvious first threat. Opposite him at wide receiver is Jalen Coker, an equally dangerous receiver whose precise routes and excellent hands make him a threat all over the field. After that is tight end Darren Waller. Each of them get a first down on over 50% of their receptions and nearly 30% of their targets. They have accounted for eight of Young's 13 touchdowns and have season long receptions of 32 (McMIllan), 51 (Coker), and 50 (Waller) yards. You'll hear their names often if Young can get more than two seconds between snap and pressure on the average down. Such was not the case in the team's last two consecutive losses.

MHR – The Panthers' third-down efficiency has been less than optimal, mostly due to O-line play. In just seven games, Young has been sacked almost as many times as he was in 2025. How will the Panthers try to remedy that against a stout Broncos' defensive line that puts a lot of pressure on the QB, both up the middle and from the edge? Which defender worries you the most?

WC: You could line up anyone of your readers opposite the Panthers offensive line and I'd be worried for Young right now. 11 sacks in two games in five days has been completely demoralizing. Injuries have played a huge part, dating back to a torn bicep in 2025 for guard Robert Hunt that he has clearly not recovered fully from. That is an excuse that can explain recent games, but it doesn't threaten to win any future ones. The one bright spot is that the Panthers longtime utility lineman, Brady Christensen, is expected to return this week from injured reserve. He can plug in at any of the five spots on the offensive line and would be expected to be an instant upgrade at all of them.

Of course, we also got RT1 Taylor Moton back from injured reserve in the Buccaneers game to much the same expectation, only for Walker to get tackled into his leg and break his femur–potentially ending his career as a Carolina Panther. Bear with us if we are not brimming with optimism at the moment.

MHR – Speaking of QB pressure, Devin Lloyd has been your X-factor on defense, especially for a unit suffering so many injuries. The fifth-year pro already has 69 tackles, 2 interceptions, and 2 sacks on the season. How will he and the defense rattle Bo Nix and our fragile offense the most?

WC: Lloyd has been excellent in everything asked of him. He can blitz, he can cover, he can stuff the run. Former defensive coordinator Ejiro Evero's scheme was often described as overly complex and Lloyd stepped in and mastered it this offseason as a marquee free agent signing. This is the Panthers' first week without Evero and the theme has apparently been to simplify the defense so that players can play faster. Will that accelerate Lloyd or nerf the benefit his previous acumen provided? I'm anxious to find out but, unfortunately, I do not have an educated guess as to which will prove true.

MHR – The Broncos' running game has been really hit or miss, with a few splash plays mixed in with a lot of short gains. But the Panthers' run defense has surrendered over 140 rushing yards per game. Which team's weak spot wins out on Sunday – our run game or your run defense?

WC: 140 rushing yards per game looks bad, but it is misleading. The Panthers held Bijan Robinson to 72 yards and Jahmyr Gibbs to 46 yards. They just also gave up 291 yards to the Chicago Bears and 258 yards to the Tampa Bay Buccaneers. When it rains, it pours in the Carolinas. Not all of those totals, good or bad, can be explained away by injuries. There are a lot of question marks to be addressed by interim defensive coordinator Todd Wash and I struggle to believe that he is going to answer all of them perfectly. I do think, however, that he is going to start by focusing on the obvious problems and selling out to stop the run. This game will be a sloppy shoot out, if the Panthers have their way, and Brady Christensen will help the team shore up Young's protection long enough to give a desperate team a chance. Who wins that? I change my mind every hour, but I do believe the Panthers, on their best day, are capable of beating the Broncos on theirs. My worry is that the Panthers best days may be behind them given the current state of their roster.

Bonus Question: What did you think of the firing of Ejiro Evero? Were the problems on defense his fault? Do you agree it was the right move?

WC: I think the Evero firing was both the right call and likely to have little effect on the season. On the one hand, he was responsible for most of the worst rushing performances surrendered in the history of our young franchise. On the other hand, they're not installing a new scheme in Week 9 and they're not set to return any of the nine defensive players set to miss the game against the Broncos who have started multiple games for them this season. When you lean on a preseason caliber roster you get preseason caliber results.The remaining eight weeks in the 2026 season could be fascinating for Panthers fans. We could watch head coach Dave Canales do anything from winning coach of the year to getting fired depending on what he does with what is left of his roster. It's such an exciting time to be us again.

#2885. Next Tour up-and-comer inches toward breakthrough victory

When it comes to the story of Ben James, the next PGA Tour prospect on the verge of breaking through, it's probably best to just stick to the numbers.

He's 23 years old and best pals with Jackson Koivun, the last young buck to bust through the entryway to Tour stardom. James has zero wins in 12 tries, but four top-4s. Those finishes have come on the back of a microwave-like ability to get hot in an instant. When he opened this week's event in Mexico with a 65 and a 64, that marked 14 rounds of 65 or better in his young Tour career. In other words, at least one mega-low round each tournament.

His explanation for it is so basic that you'll understand why we're sticking to the numbers.

"It's difficult," he said of the many deep rounds, "but like I said, keep missing in the correct spots and keeping the ball below the hole and give yourself opportunities where you can be aggressive putting."

On that putting note: James is just a nick above average with the flatstick, but that's kind of the point. The best PGA Tour players are elite with the long game and ball-striking first, then they just hit it good enough to occasionally get hot with the putter. That's what James' game is like. He rates in the 93rd percentile with the driver (on Tour), and in the 88th percentile with his irons. Importantly, there's no weakness.

Any golf sickos who have been following the PGA Tour this fall would not be surprised to see his name atop the leaderboard of the World Wide Technology Championship — he's been moving in this direction for some time now. But there's a particularly tempting array of opportunities at stake here.

If James gets it done Sunday, he will not automatically earn a Masters invite via the win, like many fall winners have done in the past. But he will earn enough world ranking points to jump into the top 50 in the world ranking for the first time in his career … precisely two months before the first top-50 cutoff for qualification into next year's first major.

A victory would also book his ticket onto the 2028 PGA Tour Championship Series, a platform he seems destined for, but a worthy checkpoint before 2027 even begins. All of this brings us to the most important number: that four-shot lead.

James will wake up Sunday with, according to DataGolf, a 73.7% win expectancy. There are Tour winners trailing him, but the numbers are the numbers: James' game is as good as any of them, and he's got the cushion.

#2884. One Possession: Sure-handed Sam Hauser leads NBA with one wild stat

One Possession: Sure-handed Sam Hauser leads NBA with one wild stat originally appeared on NBC Sports Boston

When Sam Hauser is busting out the binoculars celebration, you know the Boston Celtics are rolling.

Hauser did that after delivering a fancy one-handed dime to a cutting Derrick White as the Boston Celtics pushed their lead as high as 46 en route to a 119-80 thrashing of the Charlotte Hornets in an NBA Cup Group C battle on Friday night at Spectrum Center.

You won't need binoculars for this eye-catching number: Hauser has now played a league-best 186 minutes, 37 seconds without a turnover this season.

Hauser has been credited with a total of 204 total passes this season without a single giveaway. On Friday night, he handed out three second-quarter assists as the Celtics piled up a season-high 33 helpers (on just 38 total makes) and padded their Cup differential against the injury-riddled Hornets.

Hauser's overall stat line doesn't jump off the page this year: He's averaging 7.8 points, 2.9 rebounds, and 1.1 assists over 20.8 minutes per game. He's shooting 40.8 percent while hoisting 5.4 3-pointers per game (and still holds the NBA record for 3-point rate with 84 percent of his total shot attempts coming beyond the arc).

But his impact is undeniable. The Celtics are outscoring opponents by 19.4 points per 100 possessions in Hauser's 187 minutes on the court, and that number plummets to a mere +0.8 points per 100 possessions in his 250 minutes on the bench. A +18.6 net differential is the best mark among all Boston players.

In the 111 minutes that Hauser has shared the floor with Jayson Tatum, the Celtics are posting a sizzling 127.8 offensive rating and have outscored opponents by 26.9 points per 100 possessions. A Hauser/Tatum/White trio has posted a +52.8 net rating in 28 total minutes together.

It continues a career-long trend for Hauser of positively impacting winning by simply taking care of the basketball and spacing the floor. Hauser has ranked in the 97th percentile or higher in turnover rate in each of the last three seasons, turning the ball over on just 5.7 percent of possessions last season (35 turnovers in 1,934 minutes).

Hauser is never going to be mistaken for a point guard, but his ability to value the basketball matters when the Celtics want to dominate the possession game. He shoots when he has open looks, is a willing ball-mover when he doesn't, and good things tend to happen when he's on the court.

And every now and then he'll throw a fancy dime worthy of breaking out the binoculars.

#2883. Notre Dame knocks off Miami. Fans storm field after Fighting Irish win

Notre Dame entered Saturday needing a statement win over Miami to strengthen its College Football Playoff resume after being left out of last season's 12-team field because of a head-to-head tiebreaker loss to the Hurricanes.

The Fighting Irish held off a late Miami rally, getting a game-clinching stop and takeaway on the Hurricanes' final possession to secure a 31-24 win at Notre Dame Stadium on Saturday, Nov. 7. As Jaiden Ausberry recovered a Leonard Moore-forced fumble to end the game, fans stormed the field to celebrate Notre Dame's biggest victory of the season.

MORE: Notre Dame takes control of CFP fate with win over No. 1 Miami

The win provides Notre Dame with its first ranked win of the season after the Fighting Irish fell short of securing that feat on Oct. 17 against BYU.

"I'm happy for this university. I'm happy for these players. There's a lot of support from Notre Dame Nation. This is a special moment for them," Notre Dame coach Marcus Freeman said on NBC. "I'm so happy for our players because they've put a lot of work to achieve this.

"They're a dang good football team, and they beat a dang good football team."

Notre Dame withstood Miami's 21-14 edge in the second half behind a strong opening half from CJ Carr and a defense that limited Darian Mensah and the Hurricanes to just three first half points.

Speaking of Carr, he finished 29-of-40 passing for 319 yards and three touchdowns. He did not turn the ball over; his fifth straight game with no interceptions thrown.

With its win, which moves Freeman's squad to 8-1 on the season, Notre Dame is also expected to move up a few spots in the next installment of the CFP rankings on Tuesday after debuting right in front of the cut line for the 12-team CFP bracket at the No. 11 seed.
